Morse Index Stability of Biharmonic Maps in Critical Dimension
Alexis Michelat

Abstract
Furthering the development of Da Lio-Gianocca-Rivière’s Morse stability theory ( [ 25 ] ) that was first applied to harmonic maps between manifolds and later extended to the case of Willmore immersions in [ 29 , 31 ] , we generalise the method to the case of (intrinsic or extrinsic) biharmonic maps. In the course of the proof, we develop a novel method to prove strong energy quantization (in the space of squared-integrable functions that corresponds to the pre-dual of the Marcinkiewicz space of weakly squared-integrable functions) in a wide class of problems in geometric analysis, which allows us to recover some previous results in a unified fashion.



Mathematical subject classification :
31B30 (primary); 35J35, 35J48, 49Q10, 53A05 (secondary).

1
Introduction

1.1
Morse Stability in Geometric Analysis

Let
H
be a Hilbert manifold modelled on a Hilbert space
H_{0}
, and let
E\in C^{2}(H,\mathbb{R})
. Recall that
\varphi\in H
is a critical point of
E
if
DE(\varphi)=0
. Define a quadratic form
Q_{\varphi}
on
T_{\varphi}H\simeq H_{0}
such that for all
u\in T_{\varphi}H
,

\displaystyle Q_{\varphi}(u)=D^{2}E(\varphi)(u,u).

We define the Morse index of
\varphi
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(\varphi)=\dim\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{R}}\left(T_{\varphi}\cap\left\{u:Q_{\varphi}(u)<0\right\}\right)

while the nullity of
\varphi
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Null}_{E}(\varphi)=\dim\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{R}}\left(T_{\varphi}\cap\left\{u:Q_{\varphi}(u)=0\right\}\right).

In favourable settings (harmonic maps between manifolds, conformally invariant problems in dimension
2
, Willmore immersions, biharmonic maps, etc), the index and nullity can be defined with respect to a differential operator. Assume that there exists a non-bounded linear operator
\mathscr{L}:\mathcal{D}\left(T_{\varphi}H\right)\rightarrow T_{\varphi}H
such that

\displaystyle Q_{\varphi}(u)=\langle u,\mathscr{L}u\rangle_{H_{0}},

we deduce that the index corresponds to the number of negative eigenvalues of
\mathscr{L}
, while the nullity is equal to the dimension of the Kernel of
\mathscr{L}
. Explicitly, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, define

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}(\lambda)=\mathcal{D}\left(T_{\varphi}H\right)\cap\left\{u:\mathscr{L}u=\lambda u\right\},

then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(\varphi)=\dim\bigoplus_{\lambda<0}\mathscr{E}(\lambda),

while

\displaystyle\mathrm{Null}_{E}(\varphi)=\mathrm{dim}\,\mathscr{E}(0).

If
\left\{\varphi_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of critical points of
E
that converges strongly towards
\varphi\in E
, one can prove under suitable hypotheses that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(\varphi)\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(\varphi)\leq\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(\varphi_{k})+\mathrm{Null}_{E}(\varphi_{k})\right)\leq\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(\varphi)+\mathrm{Null}_{E}(\varphi).

More generally, in the framework of bubbling convergence in geometric analysis, we also expect that this inequality holds. The lower semi-continuity of the Morse index is typically easier to prove and according to a general principle, the energy quantization suffices to establish the lower semi-continuity of the Morse index. In the case of classical minimal surfaces, it holds thanks to the well-known
logarithmic cutoff trick
. In the setting of Almgren-Pitts theory, refer to
[
28
]
and in the case of the viscosity method for minimal surfaces, refer to
[
36
]
. On the other hand, upper semi-continuity results for the extended Morse index (equal to the sum of the Morse index and the nullity) require much more precise information on the convergence rate of critical points. See
[
5
]
in the case of construction of minimal surfaces through the Allen-Cahn functional,
[
49
,
27
]
in the case of the Almgren-Pitts functions,
[
47
,
48
]
, and
[
25
]
in the case of conformally invariant problems in dimension
2
(see also
[
12
]
).

Last year, F. Da Lio, M. Gianocca, and T. Rivière developed a new general approach to show upper semi-continuity results for the Morse index (
[
25
]
). First developed in the case of conformally invariant problems in dimension
2
(that include harmonic maps), together with Tristan Rivière, we extended the method to prove the upper semi-continuity of the Morse index of Willmore immersions (
[
29
,
31
]
). In this article, we show how to generalise the theory to biharmonic maps dimension
4
. If the energy quantization implies (under suitable assumptions) the lower semi-continuity of the Morse index, Da Lio-Gianocca-Rivière showed that the improved energy quantization implies the upper semi-continuity of the extended Morse index. For more details on this method, refer to the introduction of
[
29
]
and to Section
1.4
in the case of biharmonic maps.

1.2
Morse Stability of Biharmonic Maps

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a bounded open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and
M^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a
C^{4}
submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We consider maps
u\in W^{2,2}(\Omega,M^{m})
and define the two biharmonic energies by

\displaystyle E(\varphi)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\Delta u|^{2}dx,

(1.1)

and

\displaystyle E_{0}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\left(\Delta u\right)^{\top}\right|^{2}dx.

(1.2)

A critical point of
E
is called an (extrinsic) biharmonic map and a critical point of
E_{0}
an (intrinsic) biharmonic map. In this article, we are concerned with Morse stability results.
For all critical point
u\in W^{2,2}(B(0,1),M^{m})
of
E
, one easily shows that the second derivative
Q
is given by

\displaystyle Q_{u}(w)=\int_{\Omega}\bigg\{|\Delta w|^{2}+\langle A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u),(\Delta A_{u})(w,w)+2(\nabla A_{u})(\nabla w,w)+2\,A_{u}(\nabla w,\nabla w)+2\,A_{u}(w,\Delta w)\rangle

\displaystyle-2\langle\langle\Delta u,\nabla P_{u}\rangle,(\nabla A_{u})(w,w)+2\,A_{u}(\nabla w,w)\rangle+\langle\langle\Delta(P_{u}),\Delta u\rangle,A_{u}(w,w)\rangle\bigg\}dx.

(1.3)

Furthermore, one can show that there exists an elliptic, fourth-order differential operator
\mathscr{L}_{u}=\Delta^{2}+\mathrm{l.o.t.}
such that

\displaystyle Q_{u}(w)=\int_{\Omega}\left\langle w,\mathscr{L}_{u}w\right\rangle dx.

(1.4)

In geometric application, one would typically consider biharmonic maps from a closed—
i.e.
compact, without boundary—manifold
\Sigma^{4}
, in which case one define the index with respect to
W^{2,2}(\Sigma^{4},\mathbb{R}^{n})
variations. However, since we restrict to biharmonic maps
u:B(0,1)\rightarrow M^{m}
, we will restrict to variations that vanish on the boundary. Explicitly, we define

\displaystyle W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1),\mathbb{R}^{n})=\overline{\mathscr{D}(B(0,1),\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{W^{2,2}}

where for all
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the space of test function
\mathscr{D}(\Omega)=C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
is the space of smooth, compactly supported function, and

\displaystyle\left\|v\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{2,2}(B(0,1))}=\sqrt{\int_{B(0,1)}\left(|\nabla v|^{2}+|\nabla v|^{2}+|v|^{2}\right)dx}.

A function
v\in W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1))
can be extended by
0
as a function
\overline{v}\in W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})=H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
. Therefore, according to trace theory
H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\partial B(0,1))=H^{\frac{3}{2}}(S^{3})
, we have in the distributional sense
v=\partial_{\nu}v=0
on
\partial B(0,1)
. Now, using (
1.4
), we can define for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
the corresponding eigenspace as

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}(\lambda)=W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1))\cap\left\{u:\mathscr{L}_{u}w=\lambda\,w\right\}.

Furthermore, an easy application of the spectral theorem shows that there exists an increasing sequence
\left\{\lambda_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lambda_{k}\rightarrow\infty
and
\mathscr{E}(\lambda)=\left\{0\right\}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{\lambda_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.
The Morse index of the critical point
u
is therefore defined as the number of negative eigenvalues (counted with multiplicity):

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(u)=\dim\bigoplus_{\lambda\leq 0}\mathscr{E}(\lambda),

while the nullity is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Null}_{E}(u)=\dim\mathscr{E}(0).

Our main result is the following one.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}:B(0,1)\rightarrow(M^{m},h)
be a sequence of biharmonic maps such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}E(u_{k})<\infty.

Then, if
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
bubble converges towards
(u_{\infty},v_{1},\cdots,v_{N})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(u_{\infty})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(v_{i})\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(u_{k})\leq\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Ind}_{E}^{0}(u_{k})\leq\mathrm{Ind}_{E}^{0}(u_{\infty})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{Ind}_{E}^{0}(v_{i}),

where
\mathrm{Ind}_{E}^{0}=\mathrm{Ind}+\mathrm{Null}
.

Refer to
[
38
]
,
[
33
]
, and the introduction of
[
21
]
for the definition of bubble convergence.
We point out that as a by-product of our analysis, we show that a strong energy quantization for biharmonic maps holds. See Corollary
5.2
for more details. The proof is only made in the case of extrinsic biharmonic maps, but as one can see in
[
18
,
21
]
, the analysis is trivially modified in the case of intrinsic biharmonic maps. For example, the Pohozaev identity is unchanged, and the second derivative only changes by sub-critical terms that can be absorbed to get inequality (
1.3
).

1.3
Description of the Proof

The main core of the proof is to show that negative variations cannot be localised in neck regions, lest negative variations vanish in the limit. Using the
\varepsilon
-regularity, if
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B_{\alpha}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}}(0)
is a neck region, we deduce that for some universal constant
C<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle|x|^{2}|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{k}(x)|+|x||\nabla\varphi_{k}(x)|\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}+\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\right).

On the other hand, one easily shows that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{\varphi_{k}}(u)\geq\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\Delta u|^{2}dx-C\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\nabla\varphi_{k}|^{2}|u||\Delta u|dx-C\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\left(|\nabla\varphi_{k}|^{2}+|\Delta\varphi_{k}|\right)|\nabla u|^{2}dx

\displaystyle-C\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\left(|\Delta\varphi_{k}|^{2}+|\nabla\varphi_{k}|^{2}\right)|u|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\geq(1-\varepsilon)\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\Delta u|^{2}dx-C\int_{\Omega}\left(|\nabla\varphi_{k}|^{2}+|\Delta\varphi_{k}|\right)|\nabla u|^{2}dx-C\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\Delta\varphi_{k}|^{2}|u|^{2}dx

\displaystyle-\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\nabla\varphi_{k}|^{4}|u|^{2}dx.

(1.5)

Combining the two results, we deduce (taking
\varepsilon=1/2
)

\displaystyle Q_{\varphi_{k}}(u)\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\Delta u|^{2}dx-C\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx-C\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx

(1.6)

that we will have to improve after obtaining more precise estimates on
\varphi_{k}
.
Now, recall the two inequalities from
[
31
]
.

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
5.3
p.
94
,
[
31
]
)
.

Let
0<a<b<\infty
, let
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and assume that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\frac{15\sqrt{4+3\pi(\pi+1)}}{2}.

(1.7)

Then, for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq\left(4+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx.

(1.8)

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
0<a<b<\infty
,
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
and let
u\in W^{2,2}(\Omega)
. Then, provided that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\sup_{n\geq 1}\frac{\pi\sqrt{\big(8+204\,n(n+2)\big)+\sqrt{\big(8+204\,n(n+2)\big)^{2}+4\,n(n+2)+16\,n^{2}(n+2)^{2}}}}{\sqrt{32\,n(n+2)+16\,n^{2}(n+2)^{2}}}

(1.9)

we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq\frac{9+\dfrac{10\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}+\dfrac{\pi^{4}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{3+\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

(1.10)

Therefore, applying the pointwise estimate, for
k
large enough, we get

\displaystyle Q_{\varphi_{k}(u)}\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(4+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\rho_{k}}\right)}\right)\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\rho_{k}}\right)}-C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}+\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\right)\right)\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|u|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx

\displaystyle+\left(3-C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}+\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\right)\right)\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

(1.11)

Thanks to the energy quantization, we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}+\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\right)=0.

Although this estimate is sufficient to obtain the positivity of the second member of the right-hand side of (
1.3
), the sign of the first member is unclear. However, if we strengthen the estimate into the following Hölder-like inequality for some
0<\beta<1

\displaystyle|x|^{2}|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{k}(x)|+|x||\nabla\varphi_{k}(x)|\leq C\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}+\frac{\Lambda}{\log\left(\frac{\alpha}{\rho_{k}}\right)}\right),

then, we can prove the neck regions contribute positively to the Morse index thanks to the following weighted Poincaré estimate (provided that
\Lambda
is small enough).

Theorem 1.4

(Theorem
5.7
p.
99
,
[
31
]
)
.

Let
0<a<b<\infty
and let
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Then, for all
0<\beta<\infty
, there exists a constant
0<C_{\beta}<\infty
such that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq C_{\beta}\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}\right)dx.

(1.12)

In order to obtain this improved pointwise estimate, we adapt the dyadic analysis of
[
25
]
and
[
29
]
to two different kinds of divergence equations in dimension
4
, which poses no major technical difficulty. However, showing the smallness of
\Lambda
is equivalent to the following improved energy quantization

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}+\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\right)=0,

which is much more technical than the one obtained in
[
25
]
or
[
30
]
. In the former case, thanks to the angular energy quantization, the holomorphy of the Hopf differential furnishes the missing estimate for the radial part of the gradient, while the specific structure of the Willmore equation in divergence form (reformulated as a Jacobian system in higher dimension) allows one to obtain the improved energy quantization more directly (
[
30
]
). However, in the case of biharmonic maps, we have no holomorphic quantity at hand and contrary to Willmore surfaces, it cannot be reformulated into a purely second-order system. Our new argument is general in nature and allows us to recover energy quantization results in the case of conformally invariant Lagrangians in dimension
2
and the Yang-Mills functional. Furthermore, it was recently use in
[
26
]
, and we believe that it should be applicable to various new settings, especially when one uses Sacks-Uhlenbeck approximations (or in the viscosity method), that break the special structure giving holomorphic objects.

1.4
Da Lio-Gianocca-Rivière’s Theory

This pioneering analysis was first applied to harmonic maps, or more generally, conformally invariant problems (
[
25
]
). The proof is based on four main steps:

1.

A strong energy quantization in the Lorentz space
L^{2,1}
.
An earlier principle discovered by F. Lin and T. Rivière (
[
24
,
23
,
22
]
) shows that a bound on the
L^{2,1}
norm grants under standard assumptions an energy quantization result for a sequence of critical points of uniformly bounded energy. Indeed, the energy quantization is equivalent to the no-neck energy property. Necks are annular region that link the macroscopic map to its bubbles. The limit energy is the energy of the limit map and the bubbles if and only if the energy in neck region vanish. Explicitly, a neck region can be defined as
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B_{\alpha}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}}(0)
where
\left\{\rho_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
and
\rho_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}
. In the case of extrinsic biharmonic maps, the no-neck energy is equivalent to

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\Delta u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}=0.

(1.13)

Since the energy in neck region does not concentrate on dyadic annuli of neck regions, it is not difficult to show the weaker estimate

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\Delta u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}=0.

(1.14)

If
\left\|\Delta u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}
is uniformly bounded, the
L^{2,1}/L^{2,\infty}
duality shows that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\Delta u_{k}|^{2}dx\leq\left\|\Delta u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\left\|\Delta u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\leq C\left\|\Delta u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}

(1.15)

which implies the no-neck energy property (
1.13
) by virtue of the energy quantization in the weak Lebesgue space
L^{2,\infty}
(for a precise definition of those spaces together with
L^{2,1}
, refer to the appendix) given in (
1.14
). The proof of the
L^{2,1}
energy quantization is the most analytically relevant part of the article as it applies to a variety of geometric equations (conformally invariant problems that include harmonic maps, biharmonic maps, Yang-Mills connections) as exemplified in the recent article of F. Da Lio and T. Rivière on
p
-harmonic maps (
[
26
]
).

2.

A Hölder-type estimate in neck regions.
As we saw above, a trivial estimate

\displaystyle|x|^{2}\left(|\Delta u|+|\nabla u|^{2}\right)\leq C\qquad\text{for all}\;\,x\in\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}=B_{\frac{b}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2a}(0).

that follows from the
\varepsilon
-regularity is not enough to show that the variations localised in neck regions give a positive contribution to the second derivative. One needs to refine this estimate as

\displaystyle|x|^{2}\left(|\Delta u|+|\nabla u|^{2}\right)\leq C\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}+\frac{1}{\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\qquad\text{for all}\;\,x\in\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}

for some
\beta>0
.

3.

Rellich and Hardy-Rellich Inequalites in Neck Regions.
Those inequalities that we discussed above allow one, taking advantage of the improved estimate in the neck regions, to show that neck regions contribute positively to the second derivative. Explicitly, if
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, we show that for all biharmonic map
u:\Omega\rightarrow M^{m}
, and for all
v\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})
, we have for some
\lambda_{1}<\infty

\displaystyle Q_{u}(v)\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-\lambda_{1}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega)}\right)\right)\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\nabla v|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

\displaystyle+\left(2\pi^{2}-\lambda_{1}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega)}\right)\right)\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{C_{2\beta}}{12}-\lambda_{1}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega)}\right)\right)\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\beta}\right)dx

(1.16)

In particular for a sequence
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of biharmonic maps of bounded energy, if
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B_{\alpha}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}}(0)
is a sequence of associated neck regions, there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
such that for all
0<\alpha<\alpha_{0}
and for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the following inequality holds for all variation
v\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))
:

\displaystyle Q_{u_{k}}(v)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|\nabla v|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\rho_{k}}\right)}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{2\beta}}{12}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{\alpha}\right)^{2\beta}+\left(\frac{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}}{|x|}\right)^{2\beta}\right)dx.

(1.17)

4.

Sylvester’s Law of Inertia.
If

\displaystyle\omega_{\alpha,k}(x)=\frac{1}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{\alpha}\right)^{2\beta}+\left(\frac{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}}{|x|}\right)^{2\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\rho_{k}}\right)}\right)

is continuously extended by constant functions on
B(0,1)
, (
3
) can be written in the weaker form

\displaystyle Q_{u_{k}}(v)\geq\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|\nabla v|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx+\lambda_{0}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|v|^{2}\,\omega_{\alpha,k}\,dx

(1.18)

for some constant
0<\lambda_{0}<\infty
. Using Sylvester’s Law of Inertia, both operators
\mathscr{L}_{u_{k}}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha,k}=\omega_{\alpha,k}^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{u_{k}}
have the same Morse index. Finally, if
w_{k}\in W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1))
is a negative eigenvalue of
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha,k}
, it is easy to show that the normalisation

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}|w_{k}|^{2}\omega_{\alpha,k}\,dx=1

together with a uniform lower bound of the smallest eigenvalue shows that
\left\{w_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded is
W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1))
, and converges weakly in
W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1))
towards a negative or null eigenvalue. Finally, using the stability inequality (
1.18
), one can show that
w_{k}
is not localised in the neck region
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)
, which finally implies that it can be attached to a negative or zero variation of the limiting map
u_{\infty}
or the bubbles, which finally implies the stability inequality.

1.5
Previously Known Results

The analytic study of biharmonic maps, following the work of F. Hélein on harmonic maps (
[
14
,
15
,
16
]
), was initiated by S.-Y. A. Chang, L. Wang and P. C. Yang (
[
4
]
) in the case of the sphere-valued maps, for which one can take advantage of Noether theorem to rewrite the equation in divergence form as in the case of harmonic maps. Late work of C. Wang (
[
42
,
44
,
43
]
) continued and generalised the previous analysis (that notably included an analysis of the biharmonic heat flow), showing an energy quantization for sphere-valued map, and a regularity theorem in special cases (see also the previous contribution of P. Stzrelecki
[
40
]
). Finally, T. Lamm and T. Rivière (
[
18
]
) managed to obtain a general regularity result using Rivière’s idea on conformally invariant variational problems (
[
34
]
) that allowed them to rewrite every general biharmonic-type equation into divergence form.

Wang’s energy quantization result was later generalised later in the case of intrinsic biharmonic maps by P. Hornung and R. Moser (
[
13
]
) and finally treated in general by P. Laurain and T. Rivière (
[
21
]
; see also
[
45
]
). This is the latter approach that we adopt in this article.

1.6
New Developments in the Morse Index Stability and Future Work

It is likely that our approach, especially the general method to obtain strong energy quantization, applies to more general problem, as it was already shown in
[
26
]
in the case of
p
-harmonic maps. Furthermore, it is likely that it applies to polyharmonic maps in critical dimension too (see
[
8
,
32
]
), since one need only a Pohoazev identity, that always holds for such problems.

Acknowledgments.
I thank Tristan Rivière for his unwavering support and many fruitful discussions.

2
Second Variation of the Biharmonic Energies

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a bounded open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and
M^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a
C^{4}
submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We consider maps
u\in W^{2,2}(\Omega,M^{m})
and define the two biharmonic energies by

\displaystyle E(\varphi)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\Delta u|^{2}dx,

(2.1)

and

\displaystyle E_{0}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\left(\Delta u\right)^{\top}\right|^{2}dx.

(2.2)

For all
\delta>0
, define

\displaystyle M_{\delta}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\cap\left\{x:\mathrm{dist}(x,M^{m})<\delta\right\}.

For
\delta>0
small enough, the nearest point projection
\Pi:M_{\delta}\rightarrow M
is well-defined and a
C^{4}
map (
3.1.20

[
7
]
). For all
x\in M
, let
P(x)=\nabla\Pi(x):\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow T_{x}M
be the orthogonal projection, and
P^{N}(x)=\mathrm{Id}-\nabla\Pi_{N}(x):\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow(T_{x}M)^{N}
. Those two maps are of class
C^{3}
, and if
A
is the second fundamental form of the isometric immersion
\iota:M^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it is a
C^{2}
map and we have

\displaystyle A_{p}(X,Y)=D_{X}P(x)(Y)\quad\text{for all}\;\,p\in M^{m},\;\;\text{for all}\;\,X,Y\in T_{x}M^{m},

where
D
is the pull-back of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
on
M^{m}
. Let us recall that this is a symmetric map of
X,Y
(this fact follows easily once one defines the normal connection on
M^{m}
). Let
v\in W^{2,2}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and consider the variation

\displaystyle u_{t}=\pi_{N}(u+t\,v).

Then, as
P
is a
C^{2}
map, we have by the Taylor formula

\displaystyle u_{t}=u+t\,\nabla\Pi(u)(v)+\frac{1}{2}t^{2}\,\nabla^{2}\Pi(u)(v,v)+o(t^{2})=u+t\,w+\frac{1}{2}t^{2}d_{w}P_{u}(v)+o(t^{2})

where
w=P_{u}(v)=d\,\Pi_{u}(v)
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle E(u_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\Delta u+t\,\Delta w+\frac{1}{2}t^{2}d_{w}P_{u}(v)\right|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\Delta u\right|^{2}dx+t\int_{\Omega}\langle\Delta u,\Delta w\rangle dx+\frac{1}{2}t^{2}\int_{\Omega}\left(|\Delta w|^{2}+\langle\Delta u,\Delta\left(d_{w}P_{u}(v)\right)\rangle\right)dx.

In particular, we deduce that

\displaystyle DE(u)(w)=\int_{\Omega}\langle\Delta u,\Delta\left(P_{u}(v)\right)\rangle dx=\int_{\Omega}\langle\Delta^{2}u,P_{u}(v)\rangle dx,

which shows that
u
is biharmonic if and only if
\left(\Delta^{2}u\right)^{\top}=0
. Likewise, we have

\displaystyle D^{2}E(u)(w,w)=\int_{\Omega}\left(|\Delta w|^{2}+\langle\Delta^{2}u,d_{w}P_{u}(v)\rangle\right)dx.

Now, using the classical equation

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}u=\Delta\left(A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u)\right)+2\,\dive\left(\langle\Delta u,\nabla P_{u}\rangle\right)-\langle\Delta(P_{u}),\Delta u\rangle,

we deduce that

\displaystyle Q_{u}(w)=\int_{\Omega}\left(|\Delta w|^{2}+\langle\Delta\left(A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u)\right)+2\,\dive\left(\langle\Delta u,\nabla P_{u}\rangle\right)-\langle\Delta(P_{u}),\Delta u\rangle,A_{u}(w,w)\rangle\right)dx.

If
\Omega
is a closed Riemannian manifold or
w\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\langle\Delta\left(A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u)\right),A_{u}(w,w)\rangle dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\langle A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u),(\Delta A_{u})(w,w)+2(\nabla A_{u})(\nabla w,w)+2\,A_{u}(dw,dw)+2\,A_{u}(w,\Delta w)\rangle dx.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\langle\dive\left(\langle\Delta u,\nabla P_{u}\rangle\right),A_{u}(w,w)\rangle dx=-\int_{\Omega}\langle\langle\Delta u,\nabla P_{u}\rangle,(\nabla A_{u})(w,w)+2\,A_{u}(\nabla w,w)\rangle dx.

Therefore, we finally get

\displaystyle Q_{u}(w)=\int_{\Omega}\bigg\{|\Delta w|^{2}+\langle A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u),(\Delta A_{u})(w,w)+2(\nabla A_{u})(\nabla w,w)+2\,A_{u}(\nabla w,\nabla w)+2\,A_{u}(w,\Delta w)\rangle

\displaystyle-2\langle\langle\Delta u,\nabla P_{u}\rangle,(\nabla A_{u})(w,w)+2\,A_{u}(\nabla w,w)\rangle+\langle\langle\Delta(P_{u}),\Delta u\rangle,A_{u}(w,w)\rangle\bigg\}dx.

(2.3)

Since
A\in C^{2}(N_{\delta})
and
P\in C^{3}(N_{\delta})
, we deduce that there exists a universal constant
C<\infty
(depending only on
N
) such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} |\nabla A_{u}|+|\nabla P_{u}|&\leq C|\nabla u|\\
|\Delta A_{u}|+|\Delta P_{u}|&\leq C\left(|\Delta u|+|\nabla u|^{2}\right)\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(2.4)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left|\langle A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u),(\Delta A_{u})(w,w)\rangle\right|\leq C\left(|\nabla u|^{4}+|\nabla u|^{2}|\Delta u|\right)|w|^{2}\\
&2\left|\langle A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u),(\nabla A_{u})(\nabla w,w)\rangle\right|\leq C|\nabla u|^{3}|\nabla w||w|\leq C|\nabla u|^{4}|w|^{2}+C|\nabla u|^{2}|\nabla w|^{2}\\
&2\left|\langle A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u),A_{u}(\nabla w,\nabla w)\rangle\right|\leq C|\nabla u|^{2}|\nabla w|^{2}\\
&2\left|\langle A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u),A_{u}(w,\Delta w)\rangle\right|\leq C|\nabla u|^{2}|w||\Delta w|\\
&2\left|\langle\langle\Delta u,\nabla P_{u}\rangle,(\nabla A_{u})(w,w)\rangle\right|\leq C|\nabla u|^{2}|\Delta u||w|^{2}\\
&4\left|\langle\langle\Delta u,\nabla P_{u}\rangle,A_{u}(\nabla w,w)\rangle\right|\leq C|\nabla u||\Delta u||\nabla w||w|\leq C|\nabla u|^{2}|\Delta w||w|^{2}+|\Delta u||\nabla w|^{2}\\
&2\left|\langle\langle\Delta(P_{u}),\Delta u\rangle,A_{u}(w,w)\rangle\right|\leq C\left(|\Delta u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}|\Delta u|\right)|w|^{2}\end{aligned}\right.

(2.5)

and finally, by (
2
), (
2.4
), and (
2.5
), we deduce that

\displaystyle Q_{u}(w)\geq\int_{\Omega}|\Delta w|^{2}dx-C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}|w||\Delta w|dx-C\int_{\Omega}\left(|\nabla u|^{2}+|\Delta w|\right)|\nabla w|^{2}-C\int_{\Omega}\left(|\Delta u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}\right)|w|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\geq(1-\varepsilon)\int_{\Omega}|\Delta w|^{2}dx-C\int_{\Omega}\left(|\nabla u|^{2}+|\Delta u|\right)|\nabla w|^{2}dx-C\int_{\Omega}|\Delta u|^{2}|w|^{2}dx-\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{4}|w|^{2}dx

(2.6)

for all
0<\varepsilon<1
.

3
Properties of Harmonic Functions in Higher Dimension

3.1
Generalities and Lebesgue Space Estimates

Recall that thanks to
[
39
, p.
140
]
, the dimension of the space
\mathscr{H}_{n}
of spherical harmonics of degree
d
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}(\mathscr{H}_{n})=\binom{n+d-1}{d-1}-\binom{n+d-3}{d-1}=\binom{n+d-1}{n}-\binom{n+d-3}{n-2}=N_{d}(n).

(3.1)

Furthermore, we have for all
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\Pi_{\lambda_{n}}(\Delta)=\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{d-1}{r}\partial_{r}-\frac{\lambda_{n}}{r^{2}}

where
\lambda_{n}=n(n+d-2)
are the eigenvalues of
-\Delta_{S^{d-1}}
on
S^{d-1}
. Now, if
f
solve the equation

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}+\frac{d-1}{r}f^{\prime}-\frac{\lambda_{n}}{r^{2}}f=0,

then, making the change of variable
f(r)=Y(\log(r))
, we get

\displaystyle f^{\prime}=\frac{1}{r}Y^{\prime}

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(Y^{\prime\prime}-Y^{\prime}\right),

which implies that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=f^{\prime\prime}+\frac{d-1}{r}f^{\prime}-\frac{\lambda_{n}}{r^{2}}f=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(Y^{\prime\prime}+(d-2)\,Y^{\prime}-\lambda_{n}Y\right).

The characteristic polynomial is given by

\displaystyle P(X)=X^{2}+(d-2)X-n(n+d-2)=(X-n)(x+n+d-2),

which shows that a general harmonic function on an annulus
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
admits the following expansion

\displaystyle u(r,\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(a_{n,k}\,r^{n}+b_{n,k}\,r^{-(n+d-2)}\right)Y_{n}^{k}(\omega).

In particular, we get as the spherical harmonics are an orthogonal base of
L^{2}(S^{d-1})
(for
d\geq 5
):

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\int_{a}^{b}\left(|a_{n,k}|^{2}r^{2n}+|b_{n,k}|^{2}r^{-2(n+d-2)}+2\,a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}r^{-(d-2)}\right)r^{d-1}\,dr

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right),

where
\beta(d)=\mathscr{H}^{d-1}(S^{d-1})=\dfrac{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)}
is the volume of
S^{d-1}
, while for
d=4
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx=\pi^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{n+2}b^{2(n+2)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n+2)}\right)+2\pi^{2}|b_{0,1}|^{2}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)

\displaystyle+\pi^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{n}\frac{1}{a^{2n}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n}\right)+2\pi^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right),

and for
d=3
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx=4\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{2n+1}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+3}b^{2n+3}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+3}\right)+4\pi|b_{0,1}|^{2}\,b\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+4\pi\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{2n+1}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n-1}\frac{1}{a^{2n-1}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n-1}\right)+4\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{2n+1}a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right).

Now, let us find a lower estimate of each term in the first two series. For all
d\geq 3
, for all
n\geq 0
(such that
(d,n)\notin\left\{(3,0),(4,0)\right\}
), and for all
0<\varepsilon<1
we have

\displaystyle\left|a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,b^{2}\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}+\frac{2n+d}{4\varepsilon}\frac{1}{b^{2n+d-4}}.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)+\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)+a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-\varepsilon)|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\left(1-\frac{(2n+d-4)(2n+d)}{4\varepsilon}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right).

(3.2)

For
(d,n)=(3,0)
, for all
0<\delta<1
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{0,1}|^{2}}{3}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{3}\right)+|b_{0,1}|^{2}\,b\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)\right)+a_{0,1}\,b_{0,1}\,b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)\geq\frac{1-\delta}{3}|a_{0,1}|^{2}b^{3}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{3}\right)

\displaystyle+|b_{0,1}|^{2}\,b\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)-\frac{3}{4\,\delta}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)\right).

(3.3)

In particular, we need to choose
\dfrac{3}{4}<\delta<1
.
Letting
X=\dfrac{a}{b}
and
\alpha=\frac{3}{4\delta}
, we get a non-trivial estimate if and only if

\displaystyle P(X)=\alpha\,X-X+1-\alpha=\alpha\left(X-1\right)\left(X-\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\right)>0,

which is equivalent since
0<X<1
to

\displaystyle\frac{a}{b}=X<\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}=\frac{1-\frac{3}{4\delta}}{\frac{3}{4\delta}}=\frac{4\delta-3}{3}.

In particular, we need to assume that
\dfrac{b}{a}>3
. For all fixed
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, choose
0<\delta<1
such that

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4\delta-3}=3+\varepsilon_{0}

which yields

\displaystyle\delta=\frac{3}{4}\frac{4+\varepsilon_{0}}{3+\varepsilon_{0}}.

Therefore, (
3.1
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{0,1}|^{2}}{3}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{3}\right)+|b_{0,1}|^{2}\,b\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)\right)+a_{0,1}\,b_{0,1}b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{12(3+\varepsilon_{0})}|a_{0,1}|^{2}b^{3}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{3}\right)

\displaystyle+|b_{0,1}|^{2}\,b\left(\frac{1}{4+\varepsilon_{0}}+\frac{3+\varepsilon_{0}}{4+\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)\right),

(3.4)

which yields a non-trivial estimate provided that

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}>3+\varepsilon_{0}.

(3.5)

Finally, for
(d,n)=(4,0)
, we trivially have for all
0<\varepsilon<1

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{0,1}|^{2}}{2}b^{4}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{4}\right)+2|b_{0,1}|^{2}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)+2\,a_{0,1}\,b_{0,1}\,b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)\geq

\displaystyle\frac{(1-\varepsilon)}{2}b^{4}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{4}\right)+2|b_{0,1}|^{2}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{\varepsilon\,\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)\right).

Therefore, we get the condition

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)>\frac{1}{\varepsilon}.

Now, for general
d\geq 3
,
n\geq 0
such that
(d,n)\notin\left\{(3,0),(4,0)\right\}
, let us find a condition on the conformal class so that we do not get a trivial estimate. Let
\alpha=\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)
and

\displaystyle f(x)=4\varepsilon\,e^{\alpha x}-x(x+4)

where
x=2n+d-4
. Notice that in all cases of interest, i.e. either
d=3,4
and
n\geq 1
or
d\geq 5
and
n\geq 0
, we have
x\geq 1
, so we need only study
f
on
[1,\infty[
. We have

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(x)=4\varepsilon\alpha\,e^{\alpha x}-2(x+2)

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(x)=4\varepsilon\alpha^{2}\,e^{\alpha x}-2\geq 2\left(2\varepsilon\,\alpha^{2}e^{\alpha}-1\right)\quad\text{for all}\;\,x\geq 1.

Let us find an inverse of
g:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},x\mapstochar\rightarrow x^{n}e^{x}
(where
n>0
) in the form
h(x)=\alpha\,W(\beta\,x^{\gamma})
, where
W:]-e^{-1},\infty[
is the Lambert function. Notice that for
n=1
, we have
h=W
. In general, we have

\displaystyle g(h(x))=\alpha^{n}W(\beta\,x^{\gamma})^{n}e^{\alpha W(\beta\,x^{\gamma})}=\alpha^{n}W(\beta\,x^{\gamma})^{n-\alpha}\beta^{\alpha}x^{\alpha\gamma},

where we used the identity
W(x)e^{W(x)}=x
. Therefore, we take
\alpha=n
,
\beta=\dfrac{1}{\alpha}=\dfrac{1}{n}
and
\gamma=\dfrac{1}{n}
, which yields

\displaystyle g^{-1}(x)=h(x)=2\,W\left(\frac{\sqrt[n]{x}}{n}\right).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\left(2\varepsilon\,\alpha^{2}e^{\alpha}-1\geq 0\right)\Longleftrightarrow\left(\alpha\geq 2\,W\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\right).

(3.6)

Notice that we can find a non-trivial solution
0<\varepsilon<1
if and only if
2\alpha^{2}e^{\alpha}\geq 1
, if

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq 2\,W\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\right)=0.5398\cdots

(3.7)

Provided that (
3.7
) is satisfied, we deduce that there exists
0<\varepsilon<1
such that (
3.6
) is satisfied. In particular
f^{\prime}
is increasing on
[1,\infty[
, and

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(1)=2\left(2\varepsilon\alpha\,e^{\alpha}-3\right)\geq 0

if and only if
\alpha\geq W\left(\frac{3}{2\varepsilon}\right)
. Therefore, assuming that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq W\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)=0.72586\cdots

there exists
0<\varepsilon<1
such that
2\varepsilon\alpha\,e^{\alpha}-3\geq 0
, which implies that for all
x\geq 1

we have
f(x)\geq f(1)=4\varepsilon\,e^{\alpha}-5>0
, provided that

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}>\frac{5}{4\varepsilon}.

Notice that

\displaystyle e^{W\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)}=\frac{3}{2\,W\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)}=2.06651\cdots

so assuming that

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}\geq\frac{9}{4},

the condition on
\varepsilon
becomes

\displaystyle\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{3}{\frac{9}{2}\,W\left(\frac{9}{4}\right)}=\frac{2}{3\,W\left(\frac{9}{4}\right)}=0.7344\cdots.

Therefore, we have for all
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle 4\varepsilon\,e^{\alpha x}-x(x+4)\geq 4\varepsilon e^{\alpha}-5\geq 3\geq 0\qquad\text{for all}\;\,x\geq 1,

that implies in particular with
\varepsilon=\frac{3}{4}
that

\displaystyle 1-\frac{x(x+4)}{3}e^{-\alpha x}\geq 1-\frac{4\varepsilon_{0}}{3}=1-\frac{8}{9\,W\left(\frac{9}{4}\right)}=0.020760\cdots>\frac{1}{50}

and finally, that

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)+\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)+a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{4(2n+d)}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)+\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{50(2n+d-4)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right).

Applying this inequality again for
\varepsilon=1
yields

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)+\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)+a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\varepsilon_{0}\right)\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)\geq\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{4(2n+d-4)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right).

Gathering the two inequalities finally yields the more symmetric

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)+\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)+a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{8(2n+d)}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)+\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{8(2n+d-4)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right).

Therefore,
f
is strictly decreasing on
\left[0,x(\varepsilon)=2\,W\left(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\varepsilon}}\right)\right]
and strictly increasing on
\left[x(\varepsilon),\infty\right[
. Therefore, we get the condition

\displaystyle 4\varepsilon e^{\alpha x(\varepsilon)}-x(\varepsilon)(x(\varepsilon)+4)>0,

or

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\frac{1}{x(\varepsilon)}\log\left(\frac{x(\varepsilon)(x(\varepsilon)+4)}{4\varepsilon}\right).

(3.8)

For example, taking
\varepsilon=\dfrac{3}{4}
, we have
x(\varepsilon)=2\,W(1)=1.134\cdots
, the condition becomes

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2W(1)}\log\left(\frac{4W(1)(W(1)+2)}{3}\right)=0.5848...

(3.9)

or

\displaystyle\dfrac{b}{a}\geq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2W(1)}\log\left(\frac{4W(1)(W(1)+2)}{3}\right)\right)=1.7946\cdots

(3.10)

which implies by (
3.1
) that

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)+\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)+a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,b^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{4(2n+d)}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\frac{(2n+d-4)(2n+d)}{3}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right).

Gathering the above results, we deduce the following theorem.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
d\geq 3
,
0<a<b<\infty
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Assume that
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
is a harmonic function, and let
\left\{a_{n,k},b_{n,k}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N},1\leq k\leq N_{d}(n)}\subset\mathbb{R}
be such that

\displaystyle u(r,\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(a_{n,k}\,r^{n}+b_{n,k}\,r^{-(n+d-2)}\right)Y_{n}^{k}(\omega),

where
Y_{n}^{k}
are spherical harmonics that give an orthogonal base of
L^{2}(S^{d-1})
such that

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.71397pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.05457pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.4484pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.27722pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0muS^{d-1}}\langle Y_{n}^{k},Y_{m}^{l}\rangle\,d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=\delta_{n,m}\delta_{k,l}\qquad\text{for all}\;\,n,m,k,l\in\mathbb{N}.

Then, for
d=3
, for all
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, provided that

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}\geq 3+\varepsilon_{0},

we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx\geq\frac{\pi\varepsilon_{0}}{3(3+\varepsilon_{0})}|a_{0,1}|^{2}b^{3}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{3}\right)+|b_{0,1}|^{2}\,b\left(\frac{1}{4+\varepsilon_{0}}+\frac{3+\varepsilon_{0}}{4+\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{2n+1}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+3}b^{2n+3}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+3}\right)+\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{2n+1}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n-1}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n-1}\right).

If
d=4
, for all

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}\geq 4,

we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx\geq\frac{\log(4)-1}{2\log(4)}b^{4}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{4}\right)+2|b_{0,1}|^{2}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\left(1-\frac{\log(4)}{\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{n+2}b^{2(n+2)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n+2)}\right)+\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{n}\frac{1}{a^{2n}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n}\right).

For all
d\geq 5
, provided that the following condition on the conformal class holds

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}\geq\frac{9}{4},

(3.11)

we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx\geq\frac{\beta(d)}{8}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta(d)}{8}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right).

(3.12)

Furthermore, assuming that for some
a\leq r\leq b

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\partial_{\nu}u\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}=0,

(3.13)

then, for all
d\geq 3
, if (
3.11
) holds, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx\geq\frac{\beta(d)}{8}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta(d)}{8}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right).

(3.14)

Furthermore, using polar coordinates, we see that

\displaystyle|\nabla u|^{2}=|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\nabla_{S^{d-1}}u|^{2}=|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\nabla_{\omega}u|^{2}.

Then, observe that for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\left\{1,\cdots,N_{d}(n)\right\}
,
l\in\left\{1,\cdots,N_{d}(m)\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}\langle\nabla_{S^{d-1}}Y_{n}^{k},\nabla_{S^{d-1}}Y_{m}^{l}\rangle d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}

\displaystyle=-\int_{S^{d-1}}\langle\Delta_{S^{d-1}}Y_{n}^{k},Y_{m}^{l}\rangle d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}

\displaystyle=n(n+d-2)\int_{S^{d-1}}\langle Y_{n}^{k},Y_{m}^{l}\rangle d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=\beta(d)\,\lambda_{n}\,\delta_{n,m}\delta_{l,k}.

(3.15)

Therefore, since

\displaystyle\partial_{r}u=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(n\,a_{n,k}\,r^{n-1}-(n+d-2)b_{n,k}\,r^{-(n+d-1)}\right)Y_{n}^{k}(\omega),

and

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\Big(n^{2}|a_{n,k}|^{2}r^{2(n-1)}+(n+d-2)^{2}r^{-2(n+d-1)}|b_{n,k}|^{2}

(3.16)

\displaystyle-2n(n+d-2)a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,r^{-d}\Big)r^{d-1}\,dr

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\int_{a}^{b}n(n+d-2)\left(|a_{n,k}|^{2}r^{2(n-1)}+|b_{n,k}|^{2}r^{-2(n+d-1)}+2\,a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}r^{-d}\right)r^{d-1}\,dr

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(n|a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2n+d-2}+(n+d-2)|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-2}}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right),

(3.17)

where we used that
n^{2}+n(n+d-2)=n(2n+d-2)
and
(n+d-2)^{2}+n(n+d-2)=(n+d-2)(2n+d-2)
.
Taking
d=4
yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=2\pi^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}\left(n|a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2(n+1)}+(n+2)|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2(n+1)}}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n+1)}\right).

(3.18)

If
d\geq 3
is arbitrary, we also get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\int_{a}^{b}\left(n(2n+d-2)|a_{n,k}|^{2}r^{2(n-1)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(n+d-2)(2n+d-2)|b_{n,k}|^{2}r^{-2(n+d-1)}\right)r^{d-3}\,dr

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{n(2n+d-2)}{2n+d-4}|a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2n+d-4}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{(n+d-2)(2n+d-4)}{2n+d}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right).

(3.19)

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
d\geq 3
,
0<a<b<\infty
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Assume that
u:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is harmonic on
\Omega
. Then, for all
a\leq r<s\leq b
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq\left(\bigg(\frac{s}{b}\bigg)^{d}+\bigg(\frac{a}{r}\bigg)^{d-2}\right)\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx

(3.20)

and

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq\left(\bigg(\frac{s}{b}\bigg)^{d-2}+\bigg(\frac{a}{r}\bigg)^{d}\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

(3.21)

Proof.

We have by (
3.16
)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(n|a_{n,k}|^{2}s^{2n+d-2}+(n+d-2)|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d-2}}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\beta(d)\bigg(\frac{s}{b}\bigg)^{d}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}n|a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2n+d-2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\bigg(\frac{a}{r}\bigg)^{d-2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}(n+d-2)|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-2}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\bigg(\frac{s}{b}\bigg)^{d}+\bigg(\frac{a}{r}\bigg)^{d-2}\right)\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx.

By the exact same proof, using instead (
3.1
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq\left(\bigg(\frac{s}{b}\bigg)^{d-2}+\bigg(\frac{a}{r}\bigg)^{d}\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx,

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

This inequality can be strengthened if we impose an additional partial Dirichlet condition.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
d\geq 3
,
0<a<b<\infty
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Assume that
u:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is harmonic on
\Omega
and that
u=0
on
\partial B(0,b)
. Then, for all
a\leq r<s\leq b
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq 2\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{d-2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx

(3.22)

and for all
d\geq 5

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq\left(2+\frac{6}{d-2}+\frac{8}{(d-2)^{2}}\right)\bigg(\frac{a}{r}\bigg)^{d}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

(3.23)

If
d=3,4
, assuming that there exists
a\leq r\leq b
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\partial_{\nu}u\,d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=0,

(3.24)

we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq\left(2+\frac{6}{d-2}+\frac{8}{(d-2)^{2}}\right)\bigg(\frac{a}{r}\bigg)^{d+2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

Proof.

Since
u=0
on
\partial B(0,b)
, we deduce that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
1\leq k\leq N_{d}(n)

\displaystyle 0=\int_{\partial B(0,b)}\langle u,Y_{n}^{k}\rangle\,d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=\beta(d)\left(a_{n,k}b^{n}+b_{n,k}\,b^{-(n+d-2)}\right).

Therefore, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq k\leq N_{d}(n)
, we have

\displaystyle a_{n,k}=-b^{-(2n+d-2)}\,b_{n,k},

which implies that for all
a\leq r<s\leq b

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(n|a_{n,k}|^{2}s^{2n+d-2}+(n+d-2)|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d-2}}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(n|b_{n,k}|^{2}\left(\frac{s}{b^{2}}\right)^{2n+d-2}+(n+d-2)|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d-2}}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d-2}}\left(n+d-2+n\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{2n+d-2}\left(\frac{s}{b}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}(n+d-2)|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d-2}}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta(d)\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{d-2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}(n+d-2)|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-2}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{d-2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx.

Likewise, we have for all
d\geq 5

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx=\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{n(2n+d-2)}{2n+d-4}|a_{n,k}|^{2}s^{2n+d-4}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{(n+d-2)(2n+d-4)}{2n+d}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d}}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{n(2n+d-2)}{2n+d-4}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{s^{2n+d}}{b^{4n+2d}}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{(n+d-2)(2n+d-4)}{2n+d}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d}}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d}}\left(\frac{(n+d-2)(2n+d-4)}{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{n(2n+d-2)}{2n+d-4}\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\left(\frac{s}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(2+\frac{6}{d-2}+\frac{8}{(d-2)^{2}}\right)\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{(n+d-2)(2n+d-4)}{2n+d}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d}}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(2+\frac{6}{d-2}+\frac{8}{(d-2)^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{d}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx,

where we used that for all
n\geq 1
, the following inequality holds

\displaystyle\frac{n(2n+d-2)}{2n+d-4}\left(\frac{(n+d-2)(2n+d-4}{2n+d-4}\right)^{-1}=\frac{n}{n+d-2}\frac{2n+d-2}{2n+d-4}\frac{2n+d}{2n+d-4}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{n+d-2}\left(1+\frac{2}{2n+d-4}\right)\left(1+\frac{4}{2n+d-4}\right)\leq\left(1+\frac{2}{d-2}\right)\left(1+\frac{4}{d-2}\right).

The final inequality for
d=3,4
follows identically.
Therefore, the argument is complete thanks to the proof of Lemma
3.2
.
∎

In particular, we get

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq 2\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{d-2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx

which shows that for all
d\geq 3
(without the extra condition (
3.24
) on
u
)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq 2\frac{a^{d-2}}{r^{d}}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{d}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

(3.25)

The final lemma is elementary.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
d\geq 3
,
0<b<\infty
and
u:B(0,b)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a harmonic function function such that
\nabla u\in L^{2}(B(0,b))
. Then, for all
0\leq r\leq b
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,r)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{d}\int_{B(0,b)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx.

Proof.

Since
2d-2>d-1
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\nabla(x\mapstochar\rightarrow|x|^{-(n+d-2)})\notin L^{2}(B(0,b))
, and we deduce that
b_{n,k}=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq k\leq N_{d}(n)
. Therefore,
u
admits the expansion

\displaystyle u(r,\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}a_{n,k}\,r^{n}\,Y_{n}^{k}(\omega).

We deduce by (
3.16
) that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,r)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}n|a_{n,k}|^{2}r^{2n+d-2}\leq\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{d}\,\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}n|a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2n+d-2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{d}\int_{B(0,b)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx,

which can also be seen directly with the mean-value formula.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
d\geq 3
,
0<b<\infty
and
u:B(0,b)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a harmonic function such that
\nabla u\in L^{2}(B(0,b))
. Then, for all
0\leq r\leq b
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,r)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{d-2}\int_{B(0,b)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,r)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{n(2n+d-2)}{2n+d-4}|a_{n,k}|^{2}r^{2n+d-4}

\displaystyle\leq\beta(d)\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{d-2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{n(2n+d-2)}{2n+d-4}|a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2n+d-4}=\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{d-2}\int_{B(0,b)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

∎

We also need to get additional estimates for
u
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
d\geq 3
,
0<a<b<\infty
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Assume that
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and that
u=0
on
\partial B(0,b)
, and provided that
d=3,4
, assume that for some
a\leq r\leq b
, there holds

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\partial_{\nu}u\,d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=0.

(3.26)

Then, for all
a\leq r<s\leq b
, if
d=3,4
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|u|^{2}dx\leq 2\frac{1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{d+2}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-2}}\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{d-2}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx,

(3.27)

while for
d\geq 5

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|u|^{2}dx\leq 2\frac{1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{d}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-4}}\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{d-4}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx,

(3.28)

Proof.

As previously,
u
admits the following expansion in
L^{2}

\displaystyle u(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(a_{n,k}|x|^{n}+b_{n,k}|x|^{-(n+d-2)}\right)Y_{n}^{k}\left(\frac{x}{|x|}\right).

(3.29)

The hypothesis
u=0
on
\partial B(0,b)
implies that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for all
1\leq k\leq N_{d}(n)

\displaystyle 0=\int_{\partial B(0,b)}\langle u,Y_{n}^{k}\rangle d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=\beta(d)\left(a_{n,k}\,b^{n}+b_{n,k}\,b^{-(n+d-2)}\right).

Therefore, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq k\leq N_{d}(n)
, we have

\displaystyle a_{n,k}=-b^{-(2n+d-2)}\,b_{n,k}.

Therefore, we have for all
a\leq r<s\leq b

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|u|^{2}dx=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}s^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d}\right)

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,s^{2}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-4}+\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\left(\frac{s}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{s^{2}}{b^{2n+d-2}}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d-4}+\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\left(\frac{s}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\left(\frac{s}{b}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2}\right)\right),

(3.30)

where for
d=3,4
,
a_{0,1}=b_{0,1}=0
due to the extra condition (
3.26
), so that the second series is well-defined. Taking
s=b
and
r=a
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}+\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-2}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right).

(3.31)

On the other hand, by (
3.1
), we have the elementary estimate

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|u|^{2}dx\leq 2\,\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{r^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{2n+d}\right).

(3.32)

Therefore, by (
3.1
) and (
3.32
), we deduce that for
d=3,4
(recall that
b_{0,1}=0
in this case)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|u|^{2}dx\leq 2\frac{1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{d+2}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-2}}\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{d-2}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx,

while for
d\geq 5

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|u|^{2}dx\leq 2\frac{1-\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{d}}{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-4}}\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{d-4}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx,

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
d\geq 3
,
0<b<\infty
and
u\in L^{2}(B(0,b))
be a harmonic function. Then, for all
0\leq r\leq b
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,r)}|u|^{2}dx\leq\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{d}\int_{B(0,b)}|u|^{2}dx.

Proof.

Since
u
is smooth in
B(0,b)
, (
3.29
) reduce to

\displaystyle u(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}a_{n,k}|x|^{n}\,Y_{n}^{k}\left(\frac{x}{|x|}\right).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,r)}|u|^{2}dx=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}r^{2n+d}\leq\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{d}\,\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}=\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{d}\int_{B(0,b)}|u|^{2}dx.

∎

Then, recall that by the Bochner formula, for all Riemannian manifold
(\Sigma,g)
, and for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{g}|d\varphi|_{g}^{2}=|\nabla^{2}\varphi|_{g}^{2}+\mathrm{Ric}_{g}(du,du)+\langle du,d\,\Delta_{g}u\rangle_{g}.

Assuming that
\Sigma
is compact and has no boundary, we get by Stokes theorem

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\Delta_{g}|d\varphi|_{g}^{2}\,d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{\Sigma}\left(|\nabla^{2}\varphi|_{g}^{2}+\mathrm{Ric}_{g}(du,du)+\langle du,d\,\Delta_{g}u\rangle_{g}\right)d\mathrm{vol}_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big(|\nabla^{2}\varphi|^{2}+\mathrm{Ric}_{g}(du,du)-(\Delta_{g}u)^{2}\Big)d\mathrm{vol}_{g},

which yields the identity

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla^{2}\varphi|_{g}^{2}\,d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{\Sigma}\Big((\Delta_{g}u)^{2}-\mathrm{Ric}_{g}(du,du)\Big)d\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

(3.33)

In particular, if
(\Sigma,g)
has positive Ricci curvature, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla^{2}\varphi|_{g}^{2}\,d\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\int_{\Sigma}(\Delta_{g}u)^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

In the special case
(\Sigma,g)=(S^{d-1},g_{0})
of the sphere equipped with the round metric, we get

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}|\nabla^{2}_{S^{d-1}}\varphi|^{2}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=\int_{S^{d-1}}\left(\left(\Delta_{S^{d-1}}\varphi\right)^{2}-(d-2)|\nabla_{S^{d-1}}\varphi|^{2}\right)d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}.

(3.34)

Now, if
u
is a harmonic function as above,
i.e.
if

\displaystyle u(r,\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(a_{n,k}\,r^{n}+b_{n,k}\,r^{-(n+d-2)}\right)Y_{n}^{k}(\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}u_{n,k}(r)\,Y_{n}^{k}(\omega)

we deduce by orthogonality of the
Y_{n}^{k}
family and the identity

\displaystyle-\Delta_{S^{d-1}}Y_{n}^{k}=n(n+d-2)\,Y_{n}^{k}=\lambda_{n}\,Y_{n}^{k}

that

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}|\nabla^{2}_{\omega}u(r,\omega)|^{2}d\,\mathscr{H}^{d-1}(\omega)=\int_{S^{d-1}}\left(\left(\Delta_{S^{d-1}}u\right)^{2}+(d-2)\langle u,\Delta_{S^{d-1}}u\rangle\right)d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}|u_{n}^{k}(r)|^{2}\left(\lambda_{n}^{2}-(d-2)\lambda_{n}\right)=\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}n(n+d-2)\left(n^{2}+(d-2)(n-1)\right)|u_{n}^{k}(r)|^{2}.

Now, we have

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}=|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\nabla_{S^{d-1}}\left(\partial_{r}u\right)\right|+\left|\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{r}\nabla_{\omega}u\right)\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}|\nabla_{S^{d-1}}^{2}u|^{2}.

We have by (
3.1
)

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\nabla_{S^{d-1}}\left(\partial_{r}u\right)\right|^{2}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}n(n+d-2)\left(n\,a_{n,k}\,r^{n-2}-(n+d-2)\,b_{n,k}\,r^{-(n+d)}\right)^{2}.

Likewise,

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}\left|\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{r}\nabla_{\omega}u\right)\right|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}n(n+d-2)\left((n-1)a_{n,k}r^{n-2}-(n+d-1)b_{n,k}r^{-(n+d)}\right)^{2}.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(n(n-1)\,a_{n,k}\,r^{n-2}+(n+d-1)(n+d-2)\,b_{n,k}\,r^{-(n+d)}\right)^{2},

and

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(n(n+d-2)(n^{2}+(d-2)(n-1))\right.

\displaystyle\left.+n^{3}(n+d-2)+n(n-1)^{2}(n+d-2)+n^{2}(n-1)^{2}\right)|a_{n,k}|^{2}r^{2(n-2)}

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(n(n+d-2)(n^{2}+(d-2)(n-1))+n(n+d-2)^{3}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+n(n+d-1)^{2}(n+d-2)+(n+d-1)^{2}(n+d-2)^{2}\right)|b_{n,k}|^{2}r^{-2(n+d)}

\displaystyle+2\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(n(n+d-2)\left(n^{2}+(d-2)(n-1)\right)-n^{2}(n+d-2)^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-n(n-1)(n+d-1)(n+d-2)+n(n-1)(n+d-1)(n+d-2)\right)\,a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,r^{-(d+2)}

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}n\left(4n^{3}+4(d-3)n^{2}+(d^{2}-7d+12)n-(d-2)(d-3)\right)|a_{n,k}|^{2}r^{2(n-2)}

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}(n+d-2)\left(4n^{3}+4(2d-3)n^{2}+(5d^{2}-15d+12)n+(d-1)^{2}(d-2)\right)|b_{n,k}|^{2}r^{-2(n+d)}

\displaystyle-2(d-2)\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}n(n+d-2)\,a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,r^{-(d+2)}.

Therefore, we deduce if
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}dx=\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\int_{a}^{b}n\left(4n^{3}+4(d-3)n^{2}+(d-3)(d-4)n-(d-2)(d-3)\right)|a_{n,k}|^{2}r^{2(n-2)}\,r^{d-1}\,dr

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\int_{a}^{b}(n+d-2)\left(4n^{3}+4(2d-3)n^{2}+(5d^{2}-15d+12)n+(d-1)^{2}(d-2)\right)|b_{n,k}|^{2}r^{-2(n+d)}\,r^{d-1}\,dr

\displaystyle-2(d-2)\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}n(n+d-2)a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,r^{-3}\,dr

\displaystyle=\beta(d)\left\{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{n\left(4n^{3}+4(d-3)n^{2}+(d-3)(d-4)n-(d-2)(d-3)\right)}{2n+d-4}[a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2n+d-4}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)\right.

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{(n+d-2)\left(4n^{3}+4(2d-3)n^{2}+(5d^{2}-15d+12)n+(d-1)^{2}(d-2)\right)}{2n+d}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)

\displaystyle\left.-(d-2)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}n(n+d-2)a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\frac{1}{a^{2}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)\right\}.

We estimate

\displaystyle\left|(d-2)n(n+d-2)\,a_{n,k}\,b_{n,k}\,\frac{1}{a^{2}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}(d-2)n^{2}|a_{n,k}|^{2}a^{2n+d-4}+\frac{1}{2}(d-2)(n+d-2)^{2}|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d}}.

Then, notice that

\displaystyle\frac{(n+d-2)\left(4n^{3}+4(2d-3)n^{2}+(5d^{2}-15d+12)n+(d-1)^{2}(d-2)\right)}{2n+d}-\frac{1}{2}(d-2)(n+d-2)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(n+d-2)}{2(2n+d)}\left(8n^{3}+2(7d-10)n^{2}+(7d^{2}-20d+16)n+(d-2)\left((d-1)^{2}+1\right)\right)

Since the function in the numerator is strictly increasing in
n
(notice that
4d^{2}-17d+18\geq 3>0
for all
d\geq 3
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\inf_{n\geq 1}\left(8n^{3}+2(7d-10)n^{2}+(7d^{2}-20d+16)n+(d-2)\left((d-1)^{2}+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=8+2(7d-10)+(7d^{2}-20d+16)+(d-2)\left((d-1)^{2}+1\right)=d^{2}(d+3).

Therefore, we finally obtain the estimate for an arbitrary conformal class
0<a<b<\infty

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}dx\geq\beta(d)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{n}{2(2n+d-4)}|a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2n+d-4}\bigg(2\left(4n^{3}+4(d-3)n^{2}+(d-3)(d-4)n\right.

\displaystyle\left.-(d-2)(d-3)\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\right)-(d-2)n(2n+d-4)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}\right)\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-4}\bigg)

\displaystyle+\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{(n+d-2)}{2(2n+d)}\left(8n^{3}+2(7d-10)n^{2}+(7d^{2}-20d+16)n+(d-2)\left((d-1)^{2}+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\times|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right).

(3.35)

3.2
Lorentz Space estimate

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
d\geq 3
. Then, there exists a universal constant
C_{d}<\infty
with the following property. For all
0<2\,a\leq b<\infty
, define
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For all
0<\alpha<1
, define
\Omega_{\alpha}=B_{\alpha\,b}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}a}(0)
, and let
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
be a harmonic function. If
d=3,4
, assume that for some
a\leq r\leq b
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\partial_{\nu}u\,d\mathscr{H}^{1}=0.

(3.36)

Then, assuming that

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}\geq\frac{9}{4},

we have for all
0<\alpha<1

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\alpha})}\leq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{C_{d}}{\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-2}}}\frac{\alpha^{\frac{d-2}{2}}}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{d-1}}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\qquad\text{for all}\;\,d=3,4\\
&\frac{C_{d}}{\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-4}}}\frac{\alpha^{\frac{d-4}{2}}}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{d-1}}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\qquad\text{for all}\;\,d\geq 5.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.37)

Proof.

Recall that for all
f\in L^{2,1}(\Omega)
(
[
30
, Appendix]
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}=4\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\mathscr{L}^{d}\left(\Omega\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>t\right\}\right)\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}dt.

For all
n\geq 1
, and for all
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{d}\left(\Omega\cap\left\{x:|x|^{n}>t\right\}\right)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\beta(d)\left(b^{d}-a^{d}\right)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,t\leq a^{n}\\
&\beta(d)\left(b^{d}-t^{\frac{d}{n}}\right)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,a^{n}<t<b^{n}\\
&0\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,t\geq b^{n}.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.38)

which implies that

\displaystyle\left\||x|^{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=4\sqrt{\beta(d)}a^{n}\sqrt{b^{d}-a^{d}}+4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\int_{a^{n}}^{b^{n}}\sqrt{b^{d}-t^{\frac{d}{n}}}\,dt\leq 4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\left(a^{n}b^{\frac{d}{2}}+b^{\frac{d}{2}}(b^{n}-a^{n})\right)

\displaystyle=4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\,b^{n+\frac{d}{2}}

and this estimate also trivially holds for
n=0
. On the other hand, we have for all
n\geq 1

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{d}\left(\Omega\cap\left\{x:\frac{1}{|x|^{n}}>t\right\}\right)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\beta(d)\left(b^{d}-a^{d}\right)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,t\leq\frac{1}{b^{n}}\\
&\beta(d)\left(\frac{1}{t^{\frac{d}{n}}}-a^{d}\right)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,\frac{1}{b^{n}}<t<\frac{1}{a^{n}}\\
&0\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,t\geq\frac{1}{a^{n}}\end{aligned}\right.,

(3.39)

which implies that for
n>\dfrac{d}{2}

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{|x|^{n}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\frac{1}{b^{n}}\sqrt{b^{d}-a^{d}}+4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\int_{\frac{1}{b^{n}}}^{\frac{1}{a^{n}}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{t^{\frac{d}{n}}}-a^{d}}dt\leq 4\pi\sqrt{2}\frac{1}{b^{n-2}}+4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\int_{\frac{1}{b^{n}}}^{\frac{1}{a^{n}}}\frac{dt}{t^{\frac{d}{2n}}}dt

\displaystyle=4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\frac{1}{b^{n-\frac{d}{2}}}+4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\frac{2n}{2n-d}\left(\frac{1}{a^{n-\frac{d}{2}}}-\frac{1}{b^{n-\frac{d}{2}}}\right)\leq 4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\frac{2n}{2n-d}\frac{1}{a^{n-\frac{d}{2}}}.

Notice that for
n+d-2>\dfrac{d}{2}
if and only if
n>\dfrac{4-d}{2}
. Therefore, there are no condition for
d\geq 5
, and for
d=3,4
, the condition is equivalent to
n\geq 1
(this is why we have made the hypothesis (
3.36
)).
For
n=\dfrac{d}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{|x|^{\frac{d}{2}}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}=4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\frac{1}{b^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sqrt{b^{d}-a^{d}}+4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\int_{\frac{1}{b^{\frac{d}{2}}}}^{\frac{1}{a^{\frac{d}{2}}}}\frac{1}{t}\sqrt{1-\left(a^{d}t\right)^{2}}\,dt=4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\frac{1}{b^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sqrt{b^{4}-a^{d}}

\displaystyle+4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\left[\log(t)-\log\left(1+\sqrt{1-t^{2}}\right)+\sqrt{1-t^{2}}\right]^{1}_{\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}

\displaystyle=4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\left(\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)+\log\left(1+\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d}}\right)\right)

which cannot be bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{|x|^{\frac{d}{2}}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}=\sqrt{\beta(d)\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}

independently of the conformal class of the annulus. Recalling that
u
admits the expansion

\displaystyle u(r,\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(a_{n,k}\,r^{n}+b_{n,k}\,r^{-(n+d-2)}\right)Y_{n}^{k}(\omega).

Then, recall that by
[
39
, Corollary
2.9
p.
144
]
(notice the
\beta(d)
shift)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}|Y_{n}^{k}(\omega)|^{2}=N_{d}(n).

(3.40)

Therefore, we have (recall that
b_{0,1}=0
for
d=3,4
)

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\alpha})}

\displaystyle\leq 4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\sqrt{N_{d}(n)}|a_{n,k}|\left(\alpha\,b\right)^{n+\frac{d}{2}}+4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\sqrt{N_{d}(n)}\frac{2(n+d-2)}{2n+d-4}|b_{n,k}|\frac{\alpha^{n+d-2-\frac{d}{2}}}{a^{n+d-2-\frac{d}{2}}}

\displaystyle=4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\sqrt{N_{d}(n)}|a_{n,k}|\left(\alpha\,b\right)^{n+\frac{d}{2}}+4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\sqrt{N_{d}(n)}\frac{2(n+d-2)}{2n+d-4}|b_{n,k}|\frac{\alpha^{n+\frac{d}{2}-2}}{a^{n+\frac{d}{2}-2}}.

Therefore, we get by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\alpha})}\leq 4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}(2n+d)N_{d}(n)\alpha^{2n+d}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+4\sqrt{\beta(d)}\left(\sum_{n=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{d\leq 4\right\}}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{n=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{d\leq 4\right\}}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{4(n+d-2)^{2}}{2n+d-4}N_{d}(n)\alpha^{2n+d-4}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Now, recall that

\displaystyle N_{d}(n)

\displaystyle=\binom{n+d-1}{d-1}-\binom{n+d-3}{d-1}=\frac{(n+d-1)!}{(d-1)!\,n!}-\frac{(n+d-3)!}{(d-1)!(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{(n+d-3)!}{(d-1)!\,n!}\left((n+d-1)(n+d-2)-n(n-1)\right)=\frac{(n+d-3)!}{(d-2)!\,n!}\left(2n+d-2\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(n+d-3)^{d-3}}{(d-2)!}\left(2n+d-2\right)

which is sharp for
d=3,4
since
N_{3}(n)=2n+1
and
N_{4}(n)=(n+1)^{2}
. In particular,
N_{d}(n)
is a polynomial of degree
d-2
. Now, notice that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a universal constant
C_{k}<\infty
such that for all
0<x<1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)^{k}x^{k}\leq\frac{C_{k}}{(1-x)^{k+1}}.

Therefore, as the polynomial
P_{d}(n)=(2n+d)N_{d}^{2}(n)
has degree
\deg(P_{d}(n))=1+2(d-2)=2d-3
, we deduce that there exists a universal constant
0<\Gamma_{1}(d)<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}(2n+d)N_{d}(n)\alpha^{2n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+d)N_{d}^{2}(n)\alpha^{2n}\leq\frac{\Gamma_{1}(d)}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{2d-2}}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{n=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{d\leq 4\right\}}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{4(n+d-2)^{2}}{(2n+d-4)}N_{d}(n)\alpha^{2n+d-4}\leq\frac{\Gamma_{1}(d)}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{2d-2}}\alpha^{2(\delta_{\left\{d=3\right\}}+\delta_{\left\{d=4\right\}})},

where
\delta
is the Dirac indicator. Recalling inequality (
3.1
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx\geq\frac{\beta(d)}{8}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta(d)}{8}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\beta(d)}{8}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d}b^{2n+d}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\frac{|b_{n,k}|^{2}}{2n+d-4}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-4}}\right)\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-2}\right)&&\qquad\text{for}\;\,d=3,4\\
&\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-4}\right)&&\qquad\text{for}\;\,d\geq 5,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.41)

where we used that
b_{0,1}=0
for
d=3,4
by assumption.

Therefore, we finally get

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\alpha})}\leq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{8\sqrt{2\,\Gamma_{1}(d)}}{\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-2}}}\frac{\alpha^{\frac{d-2}{2}}}{\left(1-\alpha^{2}\right)^{d-1}}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}&&\qquad\text{for}\;\,d=3,4\\
&\frac{8\sqrt{2\,\Gamma_{1}(d)}}{\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-4}}}\frac{\alpha^{\frac{d-4}{2}}}{\left(1-\alpha^{2}\right)^{d-1}}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}&&\qquad\text{for}\;\,d\geq 5,\end{aligned}\right.

which concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Let us now prove this estimate for the Dirichlet energy.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
d\geq 3
. Then, there exists a universal constant
C_{d}^{\prime}<\infty
with the following property. For all
0<a<b<\infty
, define
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For all
0<\alpha<1
, define
\Omega_{\alpha}=B_{\alpha\,b}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}a}(0)
, and let
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega)
be a harmonic function. Then, for all
0<\alpha<1
, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\alpha})}\leq\frac{C_{d}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{d-2}}}\frac{\alpha^{\frac{d-2}{2}}}{\left(1-\alpha^{2}\right)^{d-1}}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

(3.42)

Proof.

Recall that by (
3.16
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx=\beta(d)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}\left(n|a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2n+d-2}+(n+d-2)|b_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{a^{2n+d-2}}\right)\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n+d-2}\right).

Now, we need to estimate the gradient of spherical harmonics. It can be be done in multiple ways, but the easiest one is to use the representation formula for spherical harmonics of degree
n
(
[
6
, p.
240
]
)
