\displaystyle+\left\|A\nabla u-\nabla\left(\frac{1}{2}\gamma\log|x|\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(B_{a_{i+1}}\setminus\overline{B}_{a_{i}}(0))}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

(4.16)

In particular, we get

\displaystyle\left\|A\frac{1}{r}\nabla_{\omega}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{4}})}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\left\|A\frac{1}{r}\nabla_{\omega}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(B_{a_{i+1}}\setminus\overline{B}_{a_{i}}(0))}\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\left(\int_{B_{a_{i+1}}\setminus\overline{B}_{a_{i}}(0)}\left(|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{N}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\int_{B_{a_{i+1}}\setminus\overline{B}_{a_{i}}(0)}\left(|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=C\sqrt{N}\left(\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx.

thanks to the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for series.
∎

Now, using the
\varepsilon
-regularity proven in
[
21
, Theorem
3.3
]
, we deduce the following result.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
0<a<b<\infty
,
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
and let
u\in W^{2,2}(B(0,1),M^{m})
be an (intrinsic or extrinsic) biharmonic map. Then, there exists
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(M^{m},h)>0
and
C_{1}=C_{1}(M^{m},h)<\infty
independent of
u
and
\Omega
such that for all
x\in\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}=B_{\frac{b}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2a}(0)
, the condition

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2|x|}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(B_{2|x|}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}\leq\varepsilon_{1}

implies that

\displaystyle|x|^{2}|\nabla^{2}u(x)|+|x||\nabla u(x)|\leq C_{1}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2|x|}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(B_{2|x|}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}\right).

Proof.

Using the
\varepsilon
-regularity of
[
21
, Theorem
3.3
]
, we deduce that if
v\in W^{2,2}(B(0,2),M^{m})
is an (intrinsic or extrinsic) biharmonic map and bootstrapping on the
W^{2,p}(B(0,\frac{3}{2}))
estimate (for all
p<\infty
), we have

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}v(1)|+|\nabla v(1)|\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}\right).

Therefore, applying this identity to
v(y)=u(|x|y)
, we deduce the announced estimate.
∎

An identical scaling consideration shows that the following estimate holds (see
[
18
]
where this quantity first appeared).

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
0<a<b<\infty
,
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
and let
u\in W^{2,2}(B(0,1),M^{m})
be an (intrinsic or extrinsic) biharmonic map. Then, there exists
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(M^{m},h)>0
and
C_{1}(m)=C_{1}(M^{m},h)<\infty
independent of
u
and
\Omega
such that for all
x\in\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}=B_{\frac{b}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2a}(0)
, the condition

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2|x|}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2|x|}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}\leq\varepsilon_{1}

implies that

\displaystyle|x|^{2}|\nabla^{2}u(x)|+|x||\nabla u(x)|\leq C_{1}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2|x|}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2|x|}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}\right).

In particular, for a sequence
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of biharmonic maps of bounded energy, thanks to the definition of neck regions, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}+\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\leq C_{1}(m)\sup_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}<r<\alpha}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0))}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0))}\right)

and the last quantity converges to zero as
k\rightarrow\infty
and
\alpha\rightarrow 0
. In other words, we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}+\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\right)=0.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
(M^{m},h)
be a closed Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
0<a<b<\infty
be such that

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}>\frac{9}{4},

and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
. There exists universal constant
\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}(M^{m},h)>0
and
C_{2}=C_{2}(M^{m},h)
such that for all (intrinsic or extrinsic) biharmonic map
u\in W^{2,2}(\Omega,M^{m})
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{2a<r<\frac{b}{2}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0))}\right)\leq\varepsilon_{2},

(4.17)

Then, the following estimate holds true

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\leq C_{2}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}+\left\|\partial_{r}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\right)\sqrt{\int_{\Omega}\left(|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx}.

(4.18)

Proof.

Thanks to the final argument in the proof of Theorem
By the Pohozaev identity (derived below in (
5.14
)) and the co-area formula, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\alpha}}\left(|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{3}{r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}\right)dx=\int_{\alpha^{-1}a}^{\alpha\,b}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\left(|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{3}{r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}\right)d\mathscr{H}^{3}\right)dr

\displaystyle=\int_{\alpha^{-1}a}^{\alpha\,b}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\left(2\,\partial_{r}u\cdot\left(\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u\right)\right)d\mathscr{H}^{3}\right)dr

\displaystyle+\int_{\alpha^{-1}a}^{\alpha\,b}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\left(\frac{1}{r^{4}}|\Delta_{S^{3}}u|^{2}+\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}\left(\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right)d\mathscr{H}^{3}\right)dr

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\alpha}}\left(2\,\partial_{r}u\cdot\left(\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u\right)\right)dx+\int_{\Omega_{\alpha}}\left(\frac{1}{r^{4}}|\Delta_{S^{3}}u|^{2}+\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}\left(\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right)dx.

(4.19)

Now, thanks to the estimates of Theorem
4.1
, we deduce that there exists
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that the following expansions hold true:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} A\,\partial_{r}u&=\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{2}\gamma\log|x|\right)+F=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\gamma}{|x|}\\
A\,\partial_{r}^{2}u&=\partial_{r}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\gamma\log|x|\right)+G=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{2}}+G\\
A\,\partial_{r}^{3}u&=\partial_{r}^{3}\left(\frac{1}{2}\gamma\log|x|\right)+H=\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{3}}+H,\end{aligned}\right.

(4.20)

where
F\in L^{4,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})
,
G\in L^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})
,
H\in L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})
, and

\displaystyle\left\|F\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}+\left\|G\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}+\left\|H\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle A\left(\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u\right)=A\left(\frac{\gamma}{r^{3}}+H+\frac{2}{r}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\gamma}{r^{2}}+G\right)\right)=A\,H+A\left(\frac{2}{r}G\right).

We have by Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{|x|}G\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq\Gamma_{0}\left\|G\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\left\|\frac{1}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq\left(2\pi^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\Gamma_{0}\left\|G\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})},

where
\Gamma_{0}<\infty
is independent of
\Omega
. Therefore, since
A
is uniformly bounded, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right),

which shows by the
L^{\frac{4}{3},1}/L^{4,\infty}
duality that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}2\,\partial_{r}u\cdot\left(\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\partial_{r}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\left\|\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\left\|\partial_{r}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}.

(4.21)

On the other hand, since
\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}\left(\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\in L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})
and
\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\in L^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\frac{1}{r^{4}}|\Delta_{S^{3}}u|^{2}+\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}\left(\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right)dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}\left(\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\infty)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\left(\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}+\left\|\partial_{r}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\right).

(4.22)

Using the Pohoazev identity (
4
) and the estimates (
4
), (
4
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{3}{r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}\right)dx\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\left(\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}+\left\|\partial_{r}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\right),

which concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

However, as we have pointed out in the introduction, this estimate is not sufficient to obtain the Morse stability of biharmonic maps. Indeed, we could only bound the
L^{2}
norm of
\nabla^{2}u
, but could not find an
L^{2,1}
estimate for the Hessian matrix (only its non-radial part can be bounded). This is where we develop a new argument to show the energy quantization, that we apply to two other classical problems: harmonic maps and Yang-Mills connections.

5
Pohozaev Identity and Improved Energy Quantization

Since the non-biharmonic component of
u
has its
W^{2,(2,1)}
energy quantized, it is easy to see that the
L^{2,1}
energy quantization is equivalent to the following condition

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|\Delta u_{k}|}{|x|^{2}}dx=0,

which is the same condition that first appeared in
[
25
]
(see also
[
29
]
), where the condition becomes

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|\nabla u_{k}|}{|x|}dx=0,

if
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of harmonic maps from a (possibly) degenerating sequence of Riemann surfaces—or more generally, a sequence of critical points from a fixed conformally invariant Lagrangian. In the case that the Riemann surface does not degenerate, this condition follows from the
L^{2,1}
energy quantization, that is based on the holomorphy of the Hopf differential. However, in dimension
4
, there are no holomorphic objects attached to a biharmonic map, so we must find a new argument. The idea combines the Pohozaev identity and a stability lemma for Lorentz norms, proven in the appendix (Lemma
9.2
). For simplicity, let us first expose the argument in the case of conformally invariant problems in dimension
2
, which will give us a new proof of the
L^{2,1}
energy quantization in this setting. In the case of Willmore surfaces, the
L^{2,1}
energy quantization came from the
\varepsilon
-regularity and the Jacobian system in
(\vec{R},S)
which implied an
L^{\infty}
on
(\vec{R},S)
depending only on the
L^{2}
norm of the second fundamental form. However, for biharmonic maps, there does not seem to be a way to control the quantity

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\partial B(0,b)}u\,d\mathscr{H}^{3}-\int_{\partial B(0,a)}u\,d\mathscr{H}^{3}\right|

that is equivalent to the
L^{2,1}
energy quantization in the case of biharmonic maps with values in the sphere.

5.1
Harmonic Maps in Dimension Two into Riemannian Manifolds

Thanks to
[
19
]
, if
u:B(0,b)\rightarrow M^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a harmonic map (or more generally, a critical point of a conformally invariant problem), the Pohozaev identity implies that for all
0<\rho<b
,

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,\rho)}\left|\partial_{r}u\right|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{1}=\int_{\partial B(0,\rho)}\left|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u\right|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{1}.

(5.1)

Now, let
0<a<b<\infty
, and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{t}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Omega_{t}}\frac{|\nabla u|}{|x|}dx,

(5.2)

where
0<t\leq 1
and
\Omega_{t}=B_{t\,b}\setminus\overline{B}_{t^{-1}a}(0)
.
The main result of
[
19
]
shows that (provided that the natural hypothesis on the smallness of energy on dyadic annuli holds, see
[
25
]
for more details)

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq C\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

(5.3)

In particular, we have by the
L^{2,1}
/
L^{2,\infty}
duality

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\left|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u\right|}{|x|}dx\leq\left\|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\left\|\frac{1}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}=2\sqrt{\pi}\left\|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq 2\sqrt{\pi}\,C\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

(5.4)

Therefore, we need only estimate

\displaystyle\Lambda_{t}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Omega_{t}}\frac{|\partial_{r}u|}{|x|}dx.

(5.5)

We have by the co-area formula and Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle 2\pi\Lambda_{\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\partial_{r}u|}{|x|}dx=\int_{2a}^{\frac{b}{a}}\frac{1}{r}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|\partial_{r}u|d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right)dr\leq\sqrt{2\pi}\int_{2a}^{\frac{b}{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dr

(5.6)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi}\int_{2a}^{\frac{b}{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\left|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u\right|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dr.

(5.7)

Now, thanks to our Lemma
9.2
from the appendix, if
I_{\frac{1}{2}}=[2a,\frac{b}{2}]
we have

\displaystyle\left\|r\mapstochar\rightarrow\left\|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B(0,r))}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(I_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq 2\sqrt{\pi}\left\|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq 4\pi\,C\,\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

(5.8)

Therefore, using the
L^{2,1}/L^{2,\infty}
energy quantization once more, we get

\displaystyle 2\pi\Lambda_{\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}\leq\sqrt{2\pi}\left\|r\mapstochar\rightarrow\left\|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B(0,r))}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(I_{\frac{1}{2}})}\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(I_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq 4\sqrt{2}\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}\,C\,\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)},

(5.9)

which yields

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}\leq 2\sqrt{2\pi}\,C\,\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

(5.10)

and finally,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\left(2\sqrt{2\pi}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)C\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)},

(5.11)

which concludes the proof thanks to the classical
L^{2}
energy quantization from
[
19
]
.

The principle is exactly the same in all dimension, with the difference that the Pohozaev identity is more involved.

5.2
Biharmonic Maps in Dimension Four into Manifolds

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
(M^{m},h)
be a closed Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
0<a<b<\infty
be such that

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}>\frac{9}{4},

and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
. There exists universal constant
\varepsilon_{3}=\varepsilon_{3}(M^{m},h)>0
and
C_{3}=C_{3}(M^{m},h)
such that for all (intrinsic or extrinsic) biharmonic map
u\in W^{2,2}(\Omega,M^{m})
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{2a<r<\frac{b}{2}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0))}\right)\leq\varepsilon_{3},

(5.12)

Then, the following estimate holds true

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{4}})}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{4}})}\leq C_{3}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}+\left\|\partial_{r}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\right)\sqrt{\int_{\Omega}\left(|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx}

(5.13)

Proof.

Let
u:B(0,1)\rightarrow(M^{m},h)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an extrinsic biharmonic map. A proof of the Pohozaev identity for biharmonic map can be found in
[
21
]
and , but we can derive it again. Then, we have
\Delta^{2}u\perp T_{u}M^{m}
, which implies in particular that
(x\cdot\nabla u)\cdot\Delta^{2}u=0
pointwise. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle 0=\int_{B(0,R)}(x\cdot\nabla u)\cdot\Delta^{2}u\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial B(0,R)}\left(r\,\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}\left(\Delta u\right)-\partial_{r}\left(r\,\partial_{r}u\right)\cdot\Delta u\right)\,d\mathscr{H}^{3}

\displaystyle+\int_{B(0,R)}\Delta\left(x\cdot\nabla u\right)\cdot\Delta u\,dx.

We have

\displaystyle\Delta\left(x_{i}\,\partial_{x_{i}}u\right)=x_{i}\,\partial_{x_{i}}(\Delta u)+2\,\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}u

\displaystyle\Delta(x\cdot\nabla u)=x\cdot\nabla(\Delta u)+2\Delta u,

which gives

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,R)}\Delta(x\cdot\nabla u)\cdot\Delta u\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{B(0,R)}\left(2|\Delta u|^{2}+(x\cdot\nabla(\Delta u))\cdot\Delta u\right)dx=\int_{B(0,R)}\dive\left(\frac{x}{2}|\Delta u|^{2}\right)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial B(0,R)}\frac{r}{2}|\Delta u|^{2}\,d\mathscr{H}^{3}.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,R)}\left(\frac{r}{2}|\Delta u|^{2}+r\,\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}(\Delta u)-r\,\partial_{r}^{2}u\cdot\Delta u-\partial_{r}u\cdot\Delta u\right)d\mathscr{H}^{3}=0.

We have

\displaystyle\Delta u=\partial_{r}^{2}u+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}u+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u,

which implies that

\displaystyle|\Delta u|^{2}=|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{9}{r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}|\Delta_{S^{3}}u|^{2}+\frac{6}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u\cdot\partial_{r}u+\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}u\cdot\Delta_{S^{3}}u+\frac{6}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\Delta_{S^{3}}u

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(\Delta u)=\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u-\frac{3}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}u+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}(\Delta_{S^{3}}u)-\frac{2}{r^{3}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u

\displaystyle\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}(\Delta u)=\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}^{2}u-\frac{3}{r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}(\Delta_{S^{3}}u)-\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\Delta_{S^{3}}u

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}u\cdot\Delta u=|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}^{2}u+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}u\cdot\Delta_{S^{3}}u

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\Delta u=\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}^{2}u+\frac{3}{r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\Delta_{S^{3}}u.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}|\Delta u|^{2}+\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}(\Delta u)-\partial_{r}u\cdot\Delta u-\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\Delta u=\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{9}{2\,r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{2\,r^{4}}|\Delta_{S^{3}}u|^{2}+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}^{2}u

\displaystyle+\cancel{\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u\cdot\Delta_{S^{3}}u}+\cancel{\frac{3}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\Delta_{S^{3}}u}+\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}^{2}u-\frac{3}{r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}(\Delta_{S^{3}}u)-\cancel{\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\Delta_{S^{3}}u}

\displaystyle-|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}-\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}^{2}u-\cancel{\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}u\cdot\Delta_{S^{3}}u}-\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}^{2}u-\frac{3}{r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}-\cancel{\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\Delta_{S^{3}}u}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}-\frac{3}{2r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+\partial_{r}^{3}u\cdot\partial_{r}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u\cdot\partial_{r}u+\frac{1}{2\,r^{4}}|\Delta_{S^{3}}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}(\Delta_{S^{3}}u).

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,R)}\left(|\partial_{r}^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{3}{r^{2}}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}-2\,\partial_{r}u\cdot\left(\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u\right)\right)d\mathscr{H}^{3}=\int_{\partial B(0,R)}\left(\frac{1}{r^{4}}|\Delta_{S^{3}}u|^{2}+\frac{2}{r^{2}}\,\partial_{r}u\cdot\partial_{r}(\Delta_{S^{3}}u)\right)d\mathscr{H}^{3},

(5.14)

We now define

\displaystyle\Lambda=\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\partial_{r}^{2}u|}{|x|^{2}}dx.

Now, recall the controlled Taylor expansion of (
4.20
):

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} A\,\partial_{r}u&=\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{2}\gamma\log|x|\right)+F=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\gamma}{|x|}\\
A\,\partial_{r}^{2}u&=\partial_{r}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\gamma\log|x|\right)+G=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{2}}+G\\
A\,\partial_{r}^{3}u&=\partial_{r}^{3}\left(\frac{1}{2}\gamma\log|x|\right)+H=\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{3}}+H,\end{aligned}\right.

(5.15)

where
F\in L^{4,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})
,
G\in L^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})
,
H\in L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})
, and

\displaystyle\left\|F\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}+\left\|G\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}+\left\|H\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

(5.16)

We deduce by the
L^{2,1}/L^{2,\infty}
duality that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{|A\partial_{r}^{2}u-\partial_{r}^{2}\left(\frac{\gamma}{2}\log|x|\right)|}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq\left\|G\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}\left\|\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right),

while, using the estimate

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}(A,\mathrm{SO}(n))\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right),

which shows in other words that

\displaystyle\left\|A\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\left\|A^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(1+\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right),

and
we deduce that (provided that the right-hand side is small enough)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|A^{-1}\Delta\left(\frac{\gamma}{2}\log|x|\right)|}{|x|^{2}}dx\geq\frac{|\gamma|}{2}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{dx}{|x|^{4}}=\pi^{2}|\gamma|\log\left(\frac{4\,b}{a}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})}.

(5.17)

Now, we have by the co-area formula, Hölder’s inequality, and the Pohozaev identity (
5.14
)

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|\partial_{r}^{2}u|d\mathscr{H}^{3}\right)dr\leq\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sqrt{2\pi^{2}r^{3}}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|\Delta u|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dr

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{2}}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\left|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right|^{2}+2\,\partial_{r}u\left(\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}\left(\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dr.

By the previous estimates, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\in L^{2,1}(\Omega),

and using our averaging Lemma
9.2
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{2}}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}16\left|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dr

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\pi\sqrt{2}}\cdot 2\pi\sqrt{2}\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{2}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}([a,b])}

\displaystyle=8\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{2}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle A\,\partial_{r}u=\frac{\gamma}{2r}+F_{r},

(5.18)

where
F_{r}\in W^{2,\left(\frac{4}{3},1\right)}\cap W^{1,(2,1)}\cap L^{4,1}(\Omega)
, and

\displaystyle A\left(\partial_{r}^{2}u+\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}u+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)=\frac{\gamma}{r^{2}}+G,

where
G\in W^{1,\left(\frac{4}{3},1\right)}\cap L^{2,1}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle A\,\partial_{r}^{2}u=-\frac{\gamma}{2r^{2}}+G-\frac{3}{r}F_{r}.

(5.19)

This implies that

\displaystyle A\,\left(\partial_{r}^{3}u+\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right)=\frac{\gamma}{r^{3}}+\partial_{r}G+\frac{3}{r^{2}}F_{r}-\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}F_{r}-\partial_{r}A\,\partial_{r}^{2}u.

Since
\partial_{r}A\in L^{4,2}(\Omega)
and
\partial_{r}^{2}u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, we have
\partial_{r}A\,\partial_{r}^{2}u\in L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{r}A\,\partial_{r}^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)^{2}.

Since
F_{r}\in L^{4,1}(\Omega)
, and
\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\in L^{2,\infty}(\Omega)
, we have
\frac{1}{r^{2}}F_{r}\in L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}F_{r}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

Finally, since
\partial_{r}F_{r}\in L^{2,1}(\Omega)
and
\frac{1}{r}\in L^{4,\infty}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}F_{r}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

Finally, we deduce that there exists
H\in L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle\left\|H\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right),

and

\displaystyle A\left(\partial_{r}^{3}u+\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right)=\frac{\gamma}{r^{3}}+H.

(5.20)

By (
5.19
) and (
5.20
), we get

\displaystyle A\left(\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u+\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right)=H+\frac{2}{r}F_{r}\in L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega).

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u=\left(A^{-1}\frac{\gamma}{2r^{2}}+A^{-1}\frac{F_{r}}{r}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u.

In particular, we get by the generalised Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle\left\|\sqrt{\left|\partial_{r}u\cdot\left(\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u+\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right)\right|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\sqrt{|\partial_{r}u|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{8,2}(\Omega)}\left\|\sqrt{\left|\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u+\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{8}{3},2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\partial_{r}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\partial_{r}^{3}u+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}u+\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\left\|\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}+\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)}\sqrt{\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)}

and

\displaystyle\left\|\sqrt{\left|\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}u\cdot\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{S^{3}}u\right|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\left\|\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}+\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)}

\displaystyle\times\sqrt{\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)}.

Finally, using once more our averaging Lemma
9.2
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\left\|\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\left\|\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}+\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)}\sqrt{\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)},

which implies by Cauchy’s inequality that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

and concludes the proof of the
L^{2,1}
energy quantization theorem.
∎

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}:B(0,1)\rightarrow(M^{n},h)
be a sequence of (extrinsic or intrinsic) biharmonic map of uniformly bounded energy. Then, for all neck region
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B_{\alpha}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}}(0)
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\right)=0.

5.3
Yang-Mills Functional

Let
G
be a compact Lie group (that we suppose to be isometrically embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
) of Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Let
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathfrak{g})\cap W^{1,2}(\Lambda^{1}B(0,1),\mathfrak{g})
be a
1
-form with values into
\mathfrak{g}
. Its curvature is given by

\displaystyle F_{A}=dA+[A,A],

where
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
is the Lie bracket of
\mathfrak{g}
. This expression is also written by abuse of notation
F_{A}=dA+A\wedge A
. The Yang-Mills functional is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{YM}(A)=\int_{B(0,1)}|F_{A}|^{2}dx.

A stationary Yang-Mills connection satisfies the following equation (
[
41
, (2.1.6)]
,
[
37
, (VIII.5)]
) for all compactly supported vector field
X\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B(0,1),\mathbb{R}^{n})
:

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\left(|F_{A}|^{2}-4\sum_{i,j=1}^{4}\langle F_{A}(\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}X,\vec{e}_{j}),F_{A}(\vec{e}_{i},\vec{e}_{j})\rangle\right)dx=0,

where
(\vec{e}_{1},\cdots,\vec{e}_{4})
is an orthonormal basis. Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(B(0,1))
be a radial function. Choosing
X=\varphi(r)r\partial_{r}
, we get by
[
41
, (2.1.15)]
(notice that
n=4
,
c(p)=0
and
\vec{\Phi}=1
in this formula)

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\varphi^{\prime}(r)r\left(|F_{A}|^{2}-\left|\partial_{r}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}F_{A}\right|^{2}\right)dx=0,

where
\partial_{r}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}F_{A}=F_{A}\left(\partial_{r},\,\cdot\,\right)
. Using the co-area formula, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}(r)\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}r\left(|F_{A}|^{2}-4|\partial_{r}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}F_{A}|^{2}\right)d\mathscr{H}^{3}\right)dr=0.

Therefore, we deduce that the distribution

\displaystyle T(r)=r\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\left(|F_{A}|^{2}-4|\partial_{r}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}A|^{2}\right)d\mathscr{H}^{3}

satisfies the equation

\displaystyle T^{\prime}=0\qquad\text{in}\;\,\mathscr{D}^{\prime}([0,1]).

Therefore,
T
is a constant function, and since
T(0)=0
, we deduce that
T=0
identically. In other words, the following Pohozaev identity holds for all
0<r<1

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|F_{A}|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{3}=4\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|\partial_{r}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}F_{A}|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{3}.

Since

\displaystyle|F_{A}|^{2}=|\partial_{r}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}F_{A}|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\partial_{\theta}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}F_{A}|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}\sin^{2}(\theta)}|\partial_{\varphi}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}F_{A}|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}\sin^{2}(\theta)\sin^{2}(\varphi)}|\partial_{\psi}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}F_{A}|^{2}

using spherical coordinates
(r,\omega)\in(0,\infty)\times S^{3}
and a slight abuse of notations, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|\partial_{r}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}F_{A}|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{3}=\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\partial_{\omega}\mathbin{\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt}F_{A}|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{3},

which bears a striking resemblance with the Pohozaev formula for harmonic maps (or critical points of conformally invariant Lagrangians).
Now, using Rivière’s gauge construction for small
\left\|F_{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,1))}
(
[
37
, Theorem IV.
4
]
) norm on a neck region
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
, using a controlled extension
\widetilde{A}
of
A
on
B(0,1)
(see
[
21
]
for example in the setting of biharmonic maps), we deduce the existence of a
global gauge

g\in W^{2,2}(B(0,1),G)
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \left\|\nabla A^{g}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}&+\left\|A^{g}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C_{G}\sqrt{\mathrm{YM}(A)}\\
d^{\ast}A^{g}&=0\qquad\text{in}\;\,\Omega\\
\iota^{\ast}_{S^{3}}\left(\ast A^{g}\right)&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Writing
A=A^{g}
for simplicity, the Coulomb condition implies that the Yang-Mills equation becomes

\displaystyle\Delta A=d^{\ast}\left(A\wedge A\right)+[A,\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}dA]+[A,\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(A\wedge A)]\qquad\text{in}\;\,\Omega.

If
\widetilde{A}
is a controlled extension of
A
on
B(0,1)
, we deduce by the Sobolev embedding
W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{4,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\widetilde{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}+\left\|\widetilde{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,2}(B(0,1))}\leq C\sqrt{\mathrm{YM}(A)}.

Then, let
\varphi:\Lambda^{1}B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the solution of the following equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\varphi&=d^{\ast}\left(\widetilde{A}\wedge\widetilde{A}\right)+\left[\widetilde{A},\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}d\widetilde{A}\right]+\left[\widetilde{A},\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\left(\widetilde{A}\wedge\widetilde{A}\right)\right]\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
\varphi&=0\qquad\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

Thanks to the Hölder’s inequality for Lorentz spaces, we deduce that
\widetilde{A}\wedge\widetilde{A}\in L^{4,2}\cdot L^{4,2}\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{2,1}(B(0,1))
, while

\displaystyle\left[\widetilde{A},\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}d\widetilde{A}\right]\in L^{4,2}\cdot L^{2,2}\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(B(0,1))

\displaystyle\left[\widetilde{A},\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\left(\widetilde{A}\wedge\widetilde{A}\right)\right]\in L^{4,2}\cdot L^{2,1}\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{4,2}\cdot L^{2,2}\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(B(0,1)).

In other words, we have

\displaystyle\Delta\varphi\in d^{\ast}\left(L^{2,1}\right)+L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(B(0,1)).

Therefore, thanks to the Sobolev embedding
W^{1,\left(\frac{4}{3},1\right)}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, we deduce by Calderón-Zygmund estimates that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,1))}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(B(0,1))}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\widetilde{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,2}(B(0,1))}\left(\left\|d\widetilde{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}+\left\|\widetilde{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,2}(B(0,1))}+\left\|\widetilde{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,2}(B(0,1))}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\mathrm{YM}(A)\right)\mathrm{YM}(A).

Since
\psi=A-\varphi
is a harmonic function on
\Omega
, there exists a constant
\gamma\in\mathfrak{g}
such that by Theorem
3.9
and Theorem
3.11
we have for all
0<\alpha<1
small enough

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\alpha})}+\left\|\psi-\gamma\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega_{\alpha})}\leq\frac{C_{4}^{\prime}+\Gamma_{4}}{\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}}}\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{3}}\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)},

which concludes the proof since

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\left\|\nabla A\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(1+\mathrm{YM}(A)\right)\mathrm{YM}(A).

Alternatively, we could use Lemma
9.2
.
Let us also point out that another proof using a dyadic decomposition of Rivière of the
L^{2,1}
energy quantization can be found in M. Gauvrit’s Master thesis (
[
9
]
).

6
Wente-Type Inequalities in Dimension Four

6.1
Gradient Estimate

In this section, we obtain the suitable estimates that will allow us to improve the pointwise estimate of biharmonic maps in neck regions. The difficulty here is that we need to obtain two different kinds of estimates. As in
[
25
]
, we will use a dyadic argument. First, specialising the previous Lemma
3.3
and Lemma
3.4
to
d=4
, we deduce the following result.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
0<a<b<\infty
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Assume that
u:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a harmonic map on
\Omega
such that
\nabla u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and that
u=0
on
\partial B(0,b)
. Then, for all
a\leq r<s\leq b
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{s}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq 2\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx

(6.1)

If
u:B(0,b)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a harmonic function, then

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,r)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{4}\int_{B(0,b)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx.

(6.2)

Before proving the Wente-type estimate, let us recall how to estimate the first eigenvalue of the Laplacian on a ball.

Theorem 6.2
.

For all
d\geq 2
, let
B(0,1)=B_{d}(0,1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the unit ball. Then, for all
u\in W^{1,2}_{0}(B(0,1))
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}\leq\frac{1}{j_{\frac{d-2}{2},1}}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))},

where
j_{\frac{d-2}{2},1}\geq j_{0,1}=2.4048\cdots
is the first positive zero of the Bessel function of the first kind
J_{\frac{d-2}{2}}
.

Proof.

If
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, the Bessel function of the first kind
J_{\alpha}
is one of the two solutions to the ordinary differential equation (
[
46
, (1) p.38]
)

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}J_{\alpha}(r)-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{r^{2}}J_{\alpha}(r)=-J_{\alpha}(r).

(6.3)

By the standard method of the calculus of variations, we easily see that there exists a minimiser to the Dirichlet energy

\displaystyle E(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{B(0,1)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx

in
W^{1,2}_{0}(B(0,1))
, which (by standard elliptic regularity) is a smooth function on
B(0,1)
satisfying the following system of equations:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u&=\lambda_{1}u\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
u&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

(6.4)

where

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in W^{1,2}_{0}(B(0,1))\\
v\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}|\nabla v|^{2}dx}{\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}v^{2}dx}.

One could use Gidas-Ni-Nirenberg’s theorem (
[
10
]
) to show that
u
is radial, but one can proceed directly. Expand
u
using spherical harmonics as

\displaystyle u(r,\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{d}(n)}u_{n,k}(r)Y_{n}^{k}(\omega),

where
N_{d}(n)
is a polynomial of degree
d-2
. Since
-\Delta_{S^{d-1}}Y_{n}^{k}=n(n+d-2)Y_{n}^{k}
, we deduce that
u=u_{n,k}
solves the following ordinary differential equation with Dirichlet boundary conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} u^{\prime\prime}+\frac{d-1}{r}u^{\prime}-\frac{n(n+d-2)}{r^{2}}u&=-\lambda_{1}\,u\\
&u(1)=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(6.5)

Let us show that
u
can be expressed with respect to Bessel functions. We look for a solution of the form
f(r)=r^{\beta}J_{\alpha}(r)
to the system of equations

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} f^{\prime\prime}+\frac{d-1}{r}f^{\prime}-\frac{n(n+d-2)}{r^{2}}f&=-f\\
f(1)&=0\end{aligned}\right.

Noticing that if
J_{\alpha,\lambda}=J_{\alpha}(\lambda\,\cdot\,)
solves the

\displaystyle J_{\alpha,\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}J^{\prime}_{\alpha,\lambda}(r)-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{r^{2}}=-\lambda^{2}\,J_{\alpha,\lambda},

the function
g(r)=f(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}r)
will give us the solution to (
6.5
). We compute by (
6.3
)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(r)

\displaystyle=r^{\beta}\left(J_{\alpha}^{\prime}+\frac{\beta}{r}J_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(r)

\displaystyle=r^{\beta}\left(J_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{2\beta}{r}J_{\alpha}^{\prime}-\frac{\beta(\beta-1)}{r^{2}}J_{\alpha}\right)=r^{\beta}\left(\frac{2\beta-1}{r}J_{\alpha}^{\prime}+\frac{\alpha^{2}-\beta(\beta-1)}{r^{2}}J_{\alpha}-J_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{\beta}\left(\frac{2\beta-1}{r}J_{\alpha}^{\prime}+\frac{\alpha^{2}-\beta(\beta-1)}{r^{2}}J_{\alpha}\right)-f(r)

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{d-1}{r}f^{\prime}(r)-\frac{n(n+d-2)}{r^{2}}f(r)+f(r)

\displaystyle=r^{\beta}\left(\frac{2\beta+d-2}{r}J_{\alpha}^{\prime}+\frac{\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}+(d-2)\beta-\lambda_{n}}{r^{2}}J_{\alpha}\right).

We get the conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &2\beta+d-2=0\\
&\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}+(d-2)\beta-\lambda_{n}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

which yields

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \beta&=-\frac{d-2}{2}\\
\alpha&=\pm\sqrt{\frac{(d-2)^{2}}{4}+\lambda_{n}}.\end{aligned}\right.

Recall that as
r\rightarrow 0
, for all
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{\ast}=\mathbb{Z}\cap\left\{n:|n|=-n,\;\,n\neq 0\right\}

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}(r)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}\left(\frac{r}{2}\right)^{\alpha}+o(r^{\alpha}),

(6.6)

while for
\alpha=-n<0
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we get

\displaystyle J_{-n}(r)=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{\Gamma(n+1)}\left(\frac{2}{r}\right)^{n}+o(r^{-n}).

(6.7)

Therefore, we finally deduce that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \beta&=-\frac{d-2}{2}\\
\alpha&=\sqrt{\frac{(d-2)^{2}}{4}+\lambda_{n}}.\end{aligned}\right.

Indeed, For
d\in 2\mathbb{N}
, since
J_{-n}=(-1)^{n}J_{n}
, we do not find another solution, and for
d\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
(which implies in particular that
d\geq 3
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{\frac{d-2}{2}}}J_{-\frac{d-2}{2}}(r)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{d-2}{2}}}\frac{1}{r^{d-2}}+o(r^{2-d})\qquad\text{as}\;\,r\rightarrow 0,

and
\nabla(|x|^{2-d})\notin L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
d\geq 3
. Furthermore, the Bessel function of the second kind is unbounded at
0
and
u
is smooth on
B(0,1)
, so we finally deduce that
u_{n,k}
is given by

\displaystyle u_{n,k}(r)=\frac{1}{r^{\frac{d-2}{2}}}J_{\sqrt{\frac{(d-2)^{2}}{4}+n(n+d-2)}}\left(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}r\right).

We see by (
6.6
) that this is a bounded, and even real-analytic function on
B(0,1)
. The boundary condition shows that
\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}
is a positive zero of
J_{\sqrt{\frac{(d-2)^{2}}{4}+n(n+d-2)}}
. Since
\lambda_{1}
is the smallest positive eigenvalue, we deduce that if
j_{\alpha,1}>0
is the first positive zero of
J_{\alpha}
, then

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}j_{\sqrt{\frac{(d-2)^{2}}{4}+n(n+d-2)},1}^{2}.

(6.8)

Now, using a formula due to Schläfli and Gegenbauer or Watson’s generalisation (
[
46
,
15.6
; (2-3) p. 508]
), we deduce that
\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+},\alpha\rightarrow j_{\alpha,1}
is a strictly increasing function. In particular, the sequence
\left\{j_{\sqrt{\frac{(d-2)^{2}}{4}+n(n+d-2)},1}\right\}
is strictly increasing, and since (
6.5
) holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq k\leq N_{d}(n)
we deduce that
u_{n,k}=0
for all
n\geq 1
and
k\in\left\{1,\cdots,N_{d}(n)\right\}
, and that
u=u_{0,1}
is a radial function, and finally, that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=j_{\frac{d-2}{2},1}^{2}.

For
d=2
, we find

\displaystyle u(r)=J_{0}\left(j_{0,1}\,r\right)

\lambda_{1}(B_{2}(0,1))=j_{0,1}^{2}=5.7831\cdots
, where
j_{0,1}=2.4048\cdots
is the first positive zero of
J_{0}
(see
[
46
, p. 670, p. 748]
; notice that the last digit of the zeroes of Bessel function is not also accurate:
[
46
, 20.2 p. 662]
). For
d=3
,
J_{\frac{1}{2}}
is simply equal to

\displaystyle J_{\frac{1}{2}}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi x}}\sin(x),

which shows that

\displaystyle u(r)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}J_{\frac{1}{2}}(\pi\,r)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{\sin(\pi r)}{\pi r}.

In particular, we have
j_{\frac{1}{2},1}=\pi
, and
\lambda_{1}(B_{3}(0,1))=\pi^{2}
. Finally, in the case of interest of this article, for
d=4
, we have

\displaystyle u(r)=J_{1}\left(j_{1,1}r\right),

and
j_{1,1}=3.83170\cdots
(see
[
46
, p. 673, p.748]
), which yields
\lambda_{1}=j_{1,1}^{2}=14.681970\cdots
, and the growth of
j_{\alpha,1}
in
\alpha>0
shows the last inequality mentioned in the theorem.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(B(0,1))
and assume that
u\in W^{1,2}_{0}(B(0,1))\subset W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
solves the equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta u&=f\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
u&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\left\||x|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}\leq\Gamma_{1}\left\||x|\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)\log\left(e+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))},

(6.9)

where

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}=\frac{1}{j_{1,1}}\sqrt{72+\frac{2^{14}}{3^{3}\cdot 7^{2}}+192\left(\frac{1}{\log(2)}+\frac{1}{2\log^{2}(2)}\right)}=6.1824966\cdots

(6.10)

Proof.

Extend
f
by
0
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\overline{B}(0,1)
. Let
\left\{\chi_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a partition of unity such that
\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{k})\subset B_{2^{-(k-1)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Explicitly, we have
\chi_{k}\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq\chi_{k}\leq 1
and

\displaystyle 1=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\chi_{k}\qquad\text{in}\;\,B(0,1).

Then, we have the expansion

\displaystyle u_{k}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}u_{k},

where
u_{k}
solves the equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta u_{k}&=\chi_{k}f=f_{k}\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
u_{k}&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

Integrating by parts, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}dx=-\int_{B(0,1)}u_{k}\,\Delta u_{k}\,dx=-\int_{B(0,1)}u_{k}\,f_{k}\,dx\leq\left(\int_{B(0,1)}|u_{k}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{k}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Using the Poincaré inequality

\displaystyle\left\|u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}\leq\frac{1}{j_{1,1}}\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))},

we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}\leq\frac{1}{j_{1,1}}\left\|f_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}.

(6.11)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u|^{2}dx=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx,

Furthermore, we have the elementary identity

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2p}|\nabla u|^{2}dx=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{A_{k}}|x|^{2p}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq\sum_{i,j,k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

(6.12)

Since
f_{i}\subset\widetilde{A_{i}}=B_{2^{-(i-1)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i+1)}}(0)
, we have
A_{k}\subset B(0,2^{-k})\subset B(0,2^{-(i+1)})
for all
0\leq i\leq k-1
, which yields by inequality (
6.2
) of Lemma
6.1
and (
6.11
)

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,2^{-k})}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\leq 2^{4(i+1-k)}\int_{B(0,2^{-(i+1)})}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}2^{4(i+2-k)}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx.

(6.13)

Likewise, noticing that for all
i\geq k+2
,
A_{k}=B_{2^{-k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)\subset B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i-1)}(0)}
, we get by (
6.1
) of Lemma
6.1
(
6.11
)

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\leq 2\cdot 2^{2(k-i)}\int_{B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i-1)}}(0)}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\leq\frac{2}{\lambda_{1}}\,2^{2(k-i)}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx.

(6.14)

First, we estimate directly by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j={k}}^{k+1}\left(\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{j}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{j}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\leq\frac{2}{\lambda_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\lambda_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\int_{B(0,1)}\chi_{i}|f|^{2}dx\leq\frac{2}{\lambda_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\int_{B_{2^{-(k-2)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)}|f|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{2p+1}}{\lambda_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{B_{2^{-(k-2)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)}|x|^{2p}|f(x)|^{2}dx\leq 3\frac{2^{2p+1}}{\lambda_{1}}\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2p}|f(x)|^{2}dx,

(6.15)

where
\lambda_{1}=j_{1,1}^{2}=14.681970
is the first eigenvalue of the Dirichlet Laplacian on
B(0,1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Then, we estimate by virtue of Hölder’s inequality (for the Lebesgue and counting measures) for all
I,J\subset\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\int_{A_{k}}\left(\sum_{i\in I}\nabla u_{i}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{j\in J}u_{j}\right)dx

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\left(\int_{A_{k}}\left|\sum_{i\in I}\nabla u_{i}\right|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{A_{k}}\left|\sum_{j\in J}\nabla u_{j}\right|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\int_{A_{k}}\left|\sum_{i\in I}\nabla u_{i}\right|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\int_{A_{k}}\left|\sum_{j\in J}\nabla u_{j}\right|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}\in I}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{i_{2}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}\in J}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{j_{1}}\cdot\nabla u_{j_{2}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.16)

In particular, we need only estimate the
“pure”
terms of such expressions where
I=J
. For example, applying it to
I=\left\{k,k+1\right\}
and
J=\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{1,\cdots,k+1\right\}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\int_{A_{k}}\left|\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\nabla u_{i}\right|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\int_{A_{k}}\left|\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\nabla u_{j}\right|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Therefore, we only have two more integrals left to bound.
We first have

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\left|\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\nabla u_{i}\right|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left(\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{j}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\left(\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq k\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx,

(6.17)

which yields by Fubini theorem

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}k\,2^{4(i+1-k)}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}2^{4(i+1)}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{0\leq i\leq k-1\right\}}k\,2^{-2(p+2)k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}2^{4(i+1)}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx\sum_{k=i+1}^{\infty}k\,2^{-2(p+2)k}.

(6.18)

For all
-1<x<1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=i+1}^{\infty}k\,x^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(j+i+1)x^{j+i+1}=x^{i+2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j\,x^{j-1}+\frac{i+1}{1-x}x^{i+1}=\frac{x^{i+2}}{(1-x)^{2}}+\frac{i+1}{1-x}x^{i+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{i+1-i\,x}{(1-x)^{2}}x^{i+1}\leq\frac{i+1}{(1-x)^{2}}x^{i+1}.

(6.19)

Therefore, we deduce by (
6.1
) and (
6.1
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(1-2^{-2(p+2)})^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(i+1)2^{4(i+1)}2^{-2(p+2)(i+1)}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(1-2^{-2(p+2)})^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(i+1)2^{-2p(i+1)}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(1-2^{-2(p+2)})^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(i+1)2^{-2p(i+1)}\int_{B_{2^{-(i-1)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i+1)}}}|f|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(1-2^{-2(p+2)})^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\int_{B_{2^{-(i-1)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i+1)}}(0)}\left(2+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|x|^{2p}|f(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\lambda_{1}(1-2^{-2(p+2)})^{2}}\int_{B(0,1)}\left(2+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|x|^{2p}|f(x)|^{2}dx.

(6.20)

Finally, we estimate by (
6.14
)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathrm{min}\left\{i-2,j-2\right\}}2^{-2pk}\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}||\nabla u_{j}|dx

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathrm{min}\left\{i-2,j-2\right\}}2^{-2pk}\left(\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{j}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\lambda_{1}}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathrm{min}\left\{i-2,j-2\right\}}2^{-2pk}2^{2(k+1)-(i+j)}\left(\int_{A_{i-1}\cup A_{i}}|f|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{A_{j-1}\cup A_{j}}|f|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{8}{\lambda_{1}}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}2^{-(i+j)}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{i-1}\cup A_{i})}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{j-1}\cup A_{j})}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathrm{min}\left\{i-2,j-2\right\}}2^{-2(p-1)k}.

For
p=1
, we get by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{\lambda_{1}}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\mathrm{min}\left\{i-1,j-1\right\}2^{-(i+j)}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{i-1}\cup A_{i})}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{j-1}\cup A_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{\lambda_{1}}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}i\,2^{-i}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{i-1}\cup A_{i})}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{8}{\lambda_{1}}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i\,\log^{2}(i)}\right)\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}i^{2}\log^{2}(i)2^{-2i}\int_{B_{2^{-(i-1)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i+1)}}(0)}|f|^{2}dx\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{32}{\lambda_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\log(2)}+\frac{1}{2\log^{2}(2)}\right)\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\int_{B_{2^{-(i-1)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i+1)}}(0)}|x|^{2}\log_{2}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\log\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|f(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{64}{\lambda_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\log(2)}+\frac{1}{2\log^{2}(2)}\right)\int_{B_{(0,1/2)}}|x|^{2}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)^{2}\log^{2}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|f(x)|^{2}dx

(6.21)

where we used the series-integral comparison

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i\log^{2}(i)}\leq\frac{1}{2\log^{2}(2)}+\int_{2}^{\infty}\frac{dx}{x\log^{2}(x)}=\frac{1}{\log(2)}+\frac{1}{2\log^{2}(2)},

and the inequality

\displaystyle i\log(i)2^{-i}\leq 2|x|\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)\log\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)\qquad\text{for all}\;\,2^{-(i+1)}\leq|x|\leq 2^{-i}.

For
p>1
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{\lambda_{1}}\frac{2^{2(p-1)}}{2^{2(p-1)}-1}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{i-1}\cup A_{i})}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{\lambda_{1}}\frac{2^{2(p-1)}}{2^{2(p-1)}-1}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i\log^{2}(i)}\right)\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}i\,\log^{2}(i)2^{-2i}\int_{B_{2^{-(i-1)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i+1)}}(0)}|f|^{2}dx\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{32}{\lambda_{1}}\frac{2^{2(p-1)}}{2^{2(p-1)}-1}\left(\frac{1}{\log(2)}+\frac{1}{2\log^{2}(2)}\right)\int_{B(0,1/2)}|x|^{2}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)\log^{2}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|f(x)|^{2}dx,

(6.22)

which barely improves the previous inequality and loses the dependence on
p
. We expect that this dyadic argument is severely sub-optimal for
p>1
for this range of frequencies.
Now, we have by (
6.12
) and (
6.1
)

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2p}|\nabla u|^{2}dx=\sum_{i,j,k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle+2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx+2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle+2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle+2\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+2\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+2\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle+3\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx+3\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

(6.23)

Therefore, using (
6.1
), (
6.1
), and (
6.1
), we finally get

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq\frac{72}{\lambda_{1}}\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2}|f(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle+\frac{2^{13}}{3^{3}\cdot 7^{2}\cdot\lambda_{1}}\int_{B(0,1)}\left(2+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|x|^{2}|f(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle+\frac{192}{\lambda_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\log(2)}+\frac{1}{2\log^{2}(2)}\right)\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)^{2}\log^{2}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|f(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{1}^{2}\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)^{2}\log^{2}\left(e+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|f(x)|^{2}dx,

where

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}\left(72+\frac{2^{14}}{3^{3}\cdot 7^{2}}+192\left(\frac{1}{\log(2)}+\frac{1}{2\log^{2}(2)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{j_{1,1}^{2}}\left(72+\frac{2^{14}}{3^{3}\cdot 7^{2}}+192\left(\frac{1}{\log(2)}+\frac{1}{2\log^{2}(2)}\right)\right)=38.223\cdots

∎

Notice that up to changing the constant, the proof above works in any dimension. However, for technical reasons, we need to prove weighted estimate for

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

Using (
3.1
) and Lemma
3.5
, we get the following estimate.

Lemma 6.4
.

If
0<2\,a<b<\infty
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Assume that
u:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a harmonic map such that
\nabla u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and that
u=0
on
\partial B(0,b)
. Then, for all
a\leq r<2r\leq b
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{4}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

(6.24)

If
u:B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a harmonic function, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,r)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq\left(\frac{r}{b}\right)^{2}\int_{B(0,b)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

(6.25)

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(B(0,1))
and
u:B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta u&=f\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
u&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

Then, there exists a universal constant
\Gamma_{3}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq\Gamma_{3}^{2}\int_{B(0,1)}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)^{2}\log^{2}\left(e+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|x|^{2}|f(x)|^{2}dx.

Remark 6.6
.

It shoulds be seen as an
|x|^{2}
weighted version of the direct estimate

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq C\int_{B(0,1)}|f|^{2}dx.

Proof.

Let
\left\{\chi_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a partition of unity on
B(0,2)
as in the previous theorem. By Calderón-Zygmund estimates and the Sobolev embedding
W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{4,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,2}(B(0,1))}\leq\Gamma_{3}\left\|f_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}.

Therefore, we deduce that by (
9.6
) and (
9.21
)

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq\left\||\nabla u_{k}|^{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,1))}\left\|\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(B(0,1))}\leq 4\pi\sqrt{2}\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,2}(B(0,1))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\pi\sqrt{2}\,\Gamma_{3}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{k}|^{2}dx.

Once more, we get by (
6.12
)

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2p}|\nabla u|^{2}dx=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{A_{k}}|x|^{2p}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\leq\sum_{i,j,k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

(6.26)

but this time, we will apply the result to
p=-1
, so a discussion is in order to show that the previous proof applies. Since
f_{i}\subset\widetilde{A}_{i}=B_{2^{-(i-1)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i+1)}}
, we have
A_{k}\subset B(0,2^{-k})\subset B(0,2^{-(i+1)})
, we get by (
6.25
)

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,2^{-k})}\frac{|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq 2^{2(i+1-k)}\int_{B(0,2^{-(i+1)})}\frac{|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq 4\pi\sqrt{2}\Gamma_{3}\,2^{2(i+1-k)}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx.

Likewise, if
i\geq k+2
, we have
A_{k}=B_{2^{-k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)\subset B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i-1)}}(0)
, which implies by (
6.24
)

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\frac{|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq 2\cdot 2^{4(k-i)}\int_{B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i-1)}}(0)}\frac{|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq 8\pi\sqrt{2}\,\Gamma_{3}\,2^{4(k-i)}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx.

Now, we first estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\leq 2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\leq 2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2(p+1)k}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\int_{A_{k}}\frac{|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathbb{N}}2^{-2(p+1)k}\sum_{i=k}^{k+1}\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx\leq 3\,\Gamma_{3}^{\prime}\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2(p+1)}|f(x)|^{2}dx.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\left|\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\nabla u_{i}\right|^{2}dx\leq k\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx,

which yields by Fubini’s theorem and redoing the steps in (
6.1
), (
6.1
), (
6.1
), we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-2(p+1)k}k\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\int_{A_{k}}\frac{|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}2^{2(i+1-k)}\int_{A_{k}}|f_{i}|^{2}dx\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime\prime}\int_{B(0,1)}\left(2+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|x|^{2(p+1)}|f(x)|^{2}dx.

Finally, let us treat the last frequencies. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathrm{min}\left\{i-2,j-2\right\}}2^{-2(p+1)k}\left(\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{A_{k}}|\nabla u_{j}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathrm{min}\left\{i-2,j-2\right\}}2^{-2(p+1)k}2^{4(k+1)-2(i+j)}\left(\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{i}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{B(0,1)}|f_{j}|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}2^{-2(i+j)}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{i-1}\cup A_{i})}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{j-1}\cup A_{j})}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathrm{min}\left\{i-2,j-2\right\}}2^{2(1-p)k}.

Taking
p=-1
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2pk}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\,dx\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime\prime}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}2^{4\mathrm{min}{i-1,j-1}-2(i+j)}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{i-1}\cup A_{i})}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{j-1}\cup A_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{i-1}\cup A_{i})}\right)^{2}\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime\prime}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i\log^{2}(i)}\right)\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}i^{2}\log^{2}(i)\int_{B_{2^{-(i-1)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(i+1)}}(0)}|f|^{2}dx\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}\int_{B(0,1/2)}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)^{2}\log^{2}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|f(x)|^{2}dx,

which concludes the proof of the lemma. Taking instead
p=0
yields

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=k+2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=k+2}^{\infty}\int_{A_{k}}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{j}\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}2^{2\mathrm{min}\left\{i-1,j-1\right\}-2(i+j)}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{i-1}\cup A_{i})}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{j-1}\cup A_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{3}^{\prime}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}2^{-(i+j)}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{i-1}\cup A_{i})}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{j-1}\cup A_{j})}

so the proof of the previous theorem applies.
∎

6.2
Lebesgue Estimate for Special Divergence Structure

As in
[
25
]
and
[
29
]
, we need to refine the bound

\displaystyle|x|^{2}|\nabla^{2}u(x)|+|x||\nabla u(x)|\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

We follow the adaptation of the iteration method of
[
25
]
from
[
29
]
. We first need a replacement of the weighted Wente lemma for systems of the form
\Delta u=\dive(K)
with
K\in L^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
A_{k}=B_{2^{-k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)
, and
\widetilde{A}_{k}=A_{k}\cup\overline{A}_{k+1}\setminus\partial B(0,2^{-(k+2)})=B_{2^{-k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+2)}}(0)

Lemma 6.7

(Lemma E.
3

[
25
]
)
.

Let
0<2\,a<b<\infty
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
K\in L^{\frac{4}{3}}(B(0,1))
such that
\mathrm{supp}(K)\subset B_{2^{-j}}(0)
. Let
u:B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the solution of

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta u&=\dive(K)\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
u&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

Then, for all
0\leq k<j
, there holds

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}|u|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{3}\int_{B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-j}}(0)}|u|^{2}dx\leq\frac{8}{3}C_{\mathrm{CZ}}^{2}2^{2(k+1-j)}\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(B(0,1))}^{2}.

(6.27)

If we assume that
\mathrm{supp}(K)\subset A_{j}=B_{2^{-j}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(j+1)}}(0)
, for all
k>j
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,2^{-k})}|u|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\,2^{4(j+1-k)}\int_{B(0,2^{-j})}|u|^{2}dx\leq C_{\mathrm{CZ}}^{2}\,2^{4(j+1-k)}\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(B(0,1))}^{2},

(6.28)

where
C_{\mathrm{CZ}}
is the norm of the linear map
\Delta_{0}^{-1}\dive:L^{\frac{4}{3}}(B(0,1))\rightarrow L^{2}(B(0,1))
.

Proof.

By hypothesis, the function
u
is harmonic on the annulus
B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-j}(0)}
. Therefore, we have on
B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-j}}(0)
an expansion

\displaystyle u(r,\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}\left(a_{n,k}\,r^{n}+b_{n,k}\,r^{-(n+2)}\right)Y_{n}^{k}(\omega).

Integrating
\Delta u
on
B(0,r)
for
r>2^{-j}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\partial_{\nu}u\,d\mathscr{H}^{3}=\int_{B(0,r)}\Delta u\,dx=\int_{B(0,r)}\dive(K)dx=\int_{\partial B(0,r)}K\,d\mathscr{H}^{3}=0

since
\mathrm{supp}(K)\subset B_{2^{-j}}(0)
. Therefore, we have
b_{0,1}=0
, and we can apply Lemma
3.6
. Notice that
A_{k}=B_{2^{-k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)\subset B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-j}}(0)
if and only if
2^{-(k+1)}\geq 2^{-j}
, or
0\leq k<j
. For all
0\leq k<j
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}|u|^{2}dx\leq\frac{2}{1-\frac{1}{2^{2}}}\left(\frac{2^{-j}}{2^{-(k+1)}}\right)^{2}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}=\frac{8}{3}2^{2(k+1-j)}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}dx\leq\frac{8}{3}C_{\mathrm{CZ}}^{2}2^{2(k+1-j)}\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(B(0,1))}^{2}.

The second estimate follows directly from Lemma
3.7
.
∎

It seems difficult to prove a weighted estimate for a general
K\in L^{\frac{4}{3}}
. However, the
K
appearing for biharmonic maps has a special
L^{2}
underlying structure, and we will prove the weighted estimate for this sub-class function.

Recall that in the case of biharmonic functions, we have

\displaystyle K=2\nabla A\Delta\widetilde{u}+Aw\nabla\widetilde{u}+\nabla A(V\nabla\widetilde{u})-A\nabla(V\nabla\widetilde{u})-B\nabla\widetilde{u}

where
\widetilde{u}
is a controlled extension of
u
in the annular region
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
furnished by Theorem
9.13
. Recall that by (
4.12
), we have for all open subset
U\subset\Omega

\displaystyle\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(U)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(U)}+\left\|\nabla\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,2}(U)}\right).

(6.29)

In particular, applying it to
U=A_{k}
, and using the equivalence of the three norms on
W^{2,2}(A_{k})/\mathbb{R}
(that follows either by scaling invariance or from Theorem
9.13
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(A_{k})}^{2}\leq C\left(1+\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)^{2}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)^{2}\int_{A_{k}}\left(|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx

If
\left\{\chi_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
a partition of unity such that
\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{k})\subset\widetilde{A}_{k}=B_{2^{-(k-1)}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)
, since
0\leq\chi_{k}\leq 1
, we also have

\displaystyle\left\|\chi_{k}K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(B(0,1))}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)^{2}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\left(\left\||\nabla^{2}u|+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k}\cup A_{k+1})}\right).

(6.30)

Therefore, instead of treating a general case, we will assume that such a similar inequality holds in our theorem analogous to the weighted Wente inequality of
[
25
]
.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
K\in L^{\frac{4}{3}}(B(0,1))
and assume that there exists
f\in L^{2}(B(0,1))
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(\widetilde{A}_{k})}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\widetilde{A}_{k})}.

Let
u:B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the solution of

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta u&=\dive(K)\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
u&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

Then, there exists a universal constant
\Gamma_{2}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2}|u(x)|^{2}dx\leq\Gamma_{2}^{2}\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2}\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)^{2}\log^{2}\left(e+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)|f(x)|^{2}dx

(6.31)

Proof.

Let
\left\{\chi_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a partition of unity as in Theorem
6.3
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{k})\subset\widetilde{A}_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, we have an expansion

\displaystyle u_{k}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}u_{k},

where
u_{k}
solves the equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta u_{k}&=\dive(\chi_{k}K)=\dive(K_{k})\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
u_{k}&=0\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.
