In particular, since
0\leq\chi_{k}\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|K_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(B(0,1))}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\widetilde{A}_{k})}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}|u|^{2}dx=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{A_{k}}u_{i}\cdot u_{j}\,dx,

and

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}|x|^{2p}|u|^{2}dx=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{A_{k}}|x|^{2p}|u|^{2}dx\leq\sum_{i,j,k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-2pk}\int_{A_{k}}u_{i}\cdot u_{j}\,dx.

Thanks to the previous Lemma
6.7
, we see that the proof of Theorem
6.3
applies word by word if one replaces
\nabla u_{i}
by
u_{i}
. Therefore, the announced estimate follows.
∎

Using the two previous Wente Lemma, we will now be able to prove the Hölder-type estimates that first appeared in
[
25
]
.

7
Refined Pointwise Bounds on Biharmonic Functions in Annuli

7.1
Estimate of the Second Order Derivatives

Lemma 7.1

(Lemma F.
1

[
25
]
, Lemma
3.12

[
25
]
)
.

Let
K\in L^{\frac{4}{3}}(B(0,1),\mathbb{R}^{m})
and assume that there exists
f\in L^{2}(B(0,1))
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(A_{k})}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}.

If
u
solve the equation

\displaystyle\Delta u=\dive(K)\qquad\text{in}\;\,B(0,1).

Then, the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}(0))}\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}+\frac{5}{2}\Gamma_{2}\left\|\omega\,f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))},

where
\omega(x)=|x|\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)\log\left(e+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)
, and
\Gamma_{2}<\infty
is the universal constant of Theorem
6.8
.

Proof.

Make the decomposition
u=\varphi+\psi
, where

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\varphi&=\dive(K)\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
\varphi&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

By Theorem
6.8
, we have

\displaystyle\left\||x|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}\leq C\left\|\omega\,h\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}

Since
\psi
is harmonic on
B(0,1)
, it admits the expansion

\displaystyle\psi(r,\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}a_{n,k}\,r^{n}\,Y_{n}^{k}(\omega),

where
Y_{n}^{k}
are the spherical harmonics and
a_{n,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We have for all
0\leq a\leq b\leq 1

\displaystyle\int_{B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)}|\psi|^{2}dx=\pi^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{n+2}b^{2(n+2)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n+2)}\right).

(7.1)

Taking successively
(a,b)=\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)
and
(a,b)=\left(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}\right)
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0)}|\psi|^{2}dx=\pi^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{n+2}\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{2(n+2)}}\right)

and

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}(0)}|\psi|^{2}dx=\pi^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|a_{n,k}|^{2}}{n+2}\frac{1}{2^{2(n+2)}}\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{2(n+2)}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{16}\int_{B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0)}|\psi|^{2}dx.

(7.2)

By (
7.1
) and (
7.2
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\psi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}(0))}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}(0))}\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\|\psi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}+\frac{5}{4}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}+\frac{5}{2}\left\||x|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}+C\left\|\omega f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))},

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Likewise, the following result holds.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(B(0,1),\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
u:B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a solution of the equation

\displaystyle\Delta u=f\qquad\text{in}\;\,B(0,1).

Then, the following estimate holds true

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}(0))}\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}+\frac{5}{2}\Gamma_{2}\left\|\omega\,f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}

where
\omega(x)=|x|\left(1+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)\log\left(e+\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right)
. Likewise, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}(0))}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}+\frac{5}{2}\Gamma_{2}\left\|\omega\,f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}.

Proof.

Indeed, let
\varphi\in W^{1,2}_{0}(B(0,1))
be such that
\Delta\varphi=f
and
\psi=u-\varphi
. Then,
\psi
is a harmonic function, which implies by (
3.18
) that for all
0<a<b<\infty
.
Now, recall that by formula (
3.18
)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)}|\nabla\psi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=2\pi^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}n|a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2(n+1)}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2(n+1)}\right).

(7.3)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0)}|\nabla\psi|^{2}dx=2\pi^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}n|a_{n,k}|^{2}\left(1-\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{2(n+1)}}\right)^{2(n+1)}\right)

and

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}(0)}|\nabla\psi|^{2}dx=2\pi^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}n|a_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{2^{2(n+1)}}\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{2(n+1)}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{16}\int_{B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}}|\nabla\psi|^{2}dx.

On the other hand, using Lemma
6.5
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}\leq\Gamma_{2}\left\|\omega\,f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}.

Therefore, we finally get as in the previous lemma

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}(0))}\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0))}+\frac{5}{2}\Gamma_{2}\left\|\omega\,f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}.

Likewise, let us get the weighted estimate. We have for all
0\leq a<b\leq 1

\displaystyle\int_{B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)}\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx=2\pi^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}(n+1)|a_{n,k}|^{2}b^{2n}\left(1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2n}\right),

which implies that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx=2\pi^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}(n+1)|a_{n,k}|^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{2n}}\right)

and

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{4}}(0)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx=2\pi^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}(n+1)|a_{n,k}|^{2}\frac{1}{2^{2n}}\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{2n}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx,

which concludes the proof of the lemma by applying the exact same proof as in the first case.
∎

Theorem 7.3

(Lemma F.
2

[
25
]
, Lemma
3.13

[
29
]
)
.

Let
K\in L^{\frac{4}{3}}(B_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}(0,1),\mathbb{R}^{m})
and assume that there exists
f\in L^{2}(B(0,1))
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(A_{k})}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}.

If
u
solve the equation

\displaystyle\Delta u=\dive(K)\qquad\text{in}\;\,B(0,1).

Then, there exists a universal constant
C<\infty
such that for all
0<\alpha<1
and for all
k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq\frac{1}{4^{k}}\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{0})}+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}|f|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(7.4)

where
A_{k}=B_{2^{-k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The proof follows exactly from the one of Theorem
3.13
of
[
29
]
and we omit it.
∎

Likewise, we obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 7.4

(Lemma F.
2

[
25
]
, Lemma
3.13

[
29
]
)
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(B_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}(0,1),\mathbb{R}^{m})
and assume that
u:B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
solves the equation

\displaystyle\Delta u=f\qquad\text{in}\;\,B(0,1).

Then, there exists a universal constant
C<\infty
such that for all
0<\alpha<1
and for all
k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq\frac{1}{4^{k}}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{0})}+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}|f|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(7.5)

where
A_{k}=B_{2^{-k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Furthermore, we also have

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{0})}+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}|f|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(7.6)

7.2
Final Estimates

In order to conclude the proof, we need to prove a Hölder-type estimate for biharmonic maps in dimension
4
.

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
0<2\,a<b\leq 1
,
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
, and for all
0<t\leq 1
define
\Omega_{t}=B_{t\,b}\setminus\overline{B}_{t^{-1}a}(0)
. Let
u:\Omega\rightarrow M^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an (extrinsic) biharmonic map and define

\displaystyle\Lambda=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|A\Delta u|}{|x|^{2}}dx

(7.7)

Then, for all
0<\beta<1
, for all
0<\delta<1
there exists
C_{\beta}<\infty
and
0<\varepsilon_{\beta}(\delta)<1
such that provided that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\varepsilon_{\beta}(\delta),

then for all
x\in\Omega_{1/2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{3|x|}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{3|x|}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\left(\Lambda+\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

(7.8)

More precisely, for all
x\in\Omega_{1/2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|A\Delta u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{3|x|}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{3|x|}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi\sqrt{2\log(2)}}{\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\left(\Lambda+C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\right).

(7.9)

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{u}:B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a controlled extension of
u
given by Theorem
9.13
, and let
K
be given as in the proof of Theorem
4.1
. Make the decomposition
A\Delta\widetilde{u}=\varphi+\psi
in
\Omega
, where

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\varphi&=\dive(K)\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,1)\\
\varphi&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

(7.10)

Notice that by Calderón-Zygmund estimates, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,1))}+\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(B(0,1))}\leq C\left\|{K}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(B(0,1))}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

Since
\psi
is a harmonic function in
\Omega
, it admits the expansion

\displaystyle\psi(r,\omega)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{(n+1)^{2}}\left(a_{n,k}\,r^{n}+b_{n,k}\,r^{-(n+2)}\right)Y_{n}^{k}(\omega).

Notice that we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\partial_{\nu}\psi\,d\mathscr{H}^{3}=-4\pi^{2}\,b_{0,1}.

Writing for convenience

\displaystyle\gamma=b_{0,1}=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\partial B(0,r)}\partial_{\nu}u\,d\mathscr{H}^{3},

defining

\displaystyle\psi_{0}=\psi-\frac{\gamma}{|x|^{2}},

(7.11)

we deduce by Theorem
3.16
that for all
x\in\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}
,

\displaystyle|\psi_{0}(x)|\leq\frac{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{4}}{|x|^{2}}\left(\frac{|x|}{b}+\frac{a}{|x|}\right)\left\|\psi_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)},

(7.12)

while by Theorem
3.10
, we have for all
0<\alpha<1

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{\alpha})}\leq\frac{C_{4}}{\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}}}\frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{3}}\left\|\psi_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

(7.13)

In particular, we have for all
k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\left\|\psi_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq C\left(\frac{2^{-k}}{b}+\frac{a}{2^{-k}}\right)\left\|\psi_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

(7.14)

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{\left\|\psi_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\left\|\psi-\psi_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\psi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\left\|A\Delta u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\|\Delta u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

(7.15)

Then, by
L^{2,1}/L^{2,\infty}
duality, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\varphi|}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}\left\|\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\|{K}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)}

and

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\psi_{0}|}{|x|^{2}}dx\leq C\left(\left\|\Delta u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)}\right).

Then, notice that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\psi-\psi_{0}|}{|x|^{2}}dx=\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\gamma|}{|x|^{4}}dx=2\pi^{2}|\gamma|\log\left(\frac{4\,b}{a}\right).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle 2\pi^{2}\left||\gamma|\log\left(\frac{4\,b}{a}\right)-\Lambda\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\psi-\psi_{0}|}{|x|^{2}}dx-\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|u|}{|x|^{2}}dx\right|\leq\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|u-(\psi-\psi_{0})|}{|x|^{2}}dx=\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\varphi+\psi_{0}|}{|x|^{2}}

(7.16)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\Delta u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|K\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3},1}(\Omega)}\right)\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

(7.17)

Now, using Theorem
7.3
, we deduce that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\alpha<1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4^{k}}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{0})}+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4^{k}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(7.18)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}|\psi-\psi_{0}|^{2}dx=\int_{B_{2^{-k}}\setminus\overline{B}_{2^{-(k+1)}}(0)}\frac{|\gamma|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx=2\pi^{2}\log(2)|\gamma|^{2}.

(7.19)

Therefore, we finally get by (
7.16
), (
7.2
), and (
7.19
)

\displaystyle\left\|A\Delta\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}+\left\|\psi_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq\pi\sqrt{2\log(2)}|\gamma|+C\left(\frac{2^{-k}}{b}+\frac{a}{2^{-k}}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\pi\sqrt{2\log(2)}\left(\frac{\Lambda+\left\||\nabla^{2}u|+|x|^{-1}|\nabla u|\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}}{\log\left(\frac{4b}{a}\right)}\right)+C\left(\frac{2^{-k}}{b}+\frac{a}{2^{-k}}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(7.20)

Then, using the equation

\displaystyle\dive\left(A\nabla\widetilde{u}\right)=\nabla A\nabla\widetilde{u}+A\Delta\widetilde{u}=F,

we notice that for all open subset
U\subset B(0,1)

\displaystyle\left\|F\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(U)}\leq\left\|\nabla A\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(U)}\left\|\nabla\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(U)}+\left\|A\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(U)}\left\|\Delta\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(U)}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(U)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla\widetilde{u}}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(U)}\right).

Then, make a Hodge decomposition
A\nabla\widetilde{u}=d\chi+d^{\ast}\xi
as in
[
21
]
(
[
17
, Corollary
10.5.1
]
), where

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\chi&=F\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,\Omega\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(7.21)

and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta\xi&=dA\wedge d\widetilde{u}\qquad&&\text{in}\;\,B(0,b)\\
\xi&=0\qquad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial B(0,b).\end{aligned}\right.

(7.22)

By standard Caldrón-Zygmund estimates and interpolation theory, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}\xi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B(0,b))}+\left\|\nabla\xi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(B(0,b))}\leq C_{\alpha}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

Thanks to Theorem
7.4
, we deduce that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\alpha<1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\nabla\chi}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\left\|\frac{\nabla\chi}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{0})}+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Now, applying Theorem
7.4
, we deduce that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\alpha<1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\nabla\xi}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq\frac{1}{A_{k}}\left\|\nabla\xi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{|x|^{2}}(A_{0})}+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|\nabla A\wedge\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{2^{k}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\left\|\nabla\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,2}(A_{l})}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{2^{k}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\nabla\widetilde{u}}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq C\left\|A\nabla\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq C\left(\left\|\frac{\nabla\chi}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}+\left\|\frac{\nabla\xi}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{2^{k}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|\nabla^{2}\widetilde{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(7.23)

Now, by elliptic regularity and a scaling argument, there exists a universal constant
\Gamma_{0}<\infty
such that for all
r>0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2r}\setminus\overline{B}_{r}(0)}|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}dx\leq\int_{B_{4r}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{r}{2}}(0)}\left(|\Delta v|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla v|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx.

(7.24)

Therefore, using that

\displaystyle\left\|A\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B(0,b))}+\left\|A^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B(0,b))}\leq C\left(1+\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right),

we can rewrite (
7.2
) as

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\nabla\widetilde{u}}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq C\left\|A\nabla\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq C\left(\left\|\frac{\nabla\chi}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}+\left\|\frac{\nabla\xi}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{2^{k}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|A\Delta{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(7.25)

while (
7.2
) becomes

\displaystyle\left\|A\Delta\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq\pi\sqrt{2\log(2)}\left(\frac{\Lambda+\left\||\nabla^{2}u|+|x|^{-1}|\nabla u|\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}}{\log\left(\frac{4b}{a}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle+C\left(\frac{2^{-k}}{b}+\frac{a}{2^{-k}}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|A\Delta\widetilde{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(7.26)

Putting together (
7.2
) and (
7.2
), we finally see that

\displaystyle\left\||A\Delta\widetilde{u}|+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(A_{k})}\leq\pi\sqrt{2\log(2)}\left(\frac{\Lambda+\left\||\nabla^{2}u|+|x|^{-1}|\nabla u|\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}}{\log\left(\frac{4b}{a}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle+C\left(\frac{2^{-k}}{b}+\frac{a}{2^{-k}}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right)+\frac{C}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2\alpha|l-k+1|}}\int_{A_{l}}\left(|A\Delta\widetilde{u}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(7.27)

This is the inequality given in
[
25
, Proposition III.1]
and in
[
29
, (1.3.67)]
. Therefore, using
[
25
, Lemma 1.3.18]
, we see that the rest of the proof is identical and we omit it. The final inequality follows from (
7.24
).
∎

8
Proof of the Main Theorem

8.1
Weighted Estimate in Neck Regions

For all
0<\beta<1
, using the
\varepsilon
-regularity from
[
18
]
, we deduce that for all
x\in\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}
,

\displaystyle|x|^{2}|\Delta u(x)|+|x||\nabla u(x)|\leq C\left(\int_{B_{\frac{3|x|}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0)}\left(|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Therefore by Theorem (
7.5
), we have the following estimate for all
x\in\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{3|x|}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0))}+\left\|\frac{\nabla u}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{\frac{3|x|}{2}}\setminus\overline{B}_{\frac{|x|}{2}})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}+\frac{1}{\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega)}\right).

Combining this estimate with the
\varepsilon
-regularity yields the pointwise bound for all
x\in\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle|x|^{2}|\Delta u(x)|+|x||\nabla u(x)|\leq C\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}+\frac{1}{\log\left(\frac{b}{4a}\right)}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega)}\right)

Therefore, using (
1.3
), we deduce that there exists
C<\infty
such that for all
v\in W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle Q_{u}(v)\geq\frac{3}{4}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}|\Delta v|^{2}dx-C\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(|\Delta u|+|\nabla u|^{2}\right)|\nabla u|^{2}-C\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(|\Delta u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{4}\right)|v|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\geq\frac{3}{4}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}|\Delta v|^{2}dx-C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega)}\right)\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

\displaystyle-C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega)}\right)\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{4a}\right)}\right)dx

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-\lambda_{1}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega)}\right)\right)\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\nabla v|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

\displaystyle+\left(2\pi^{2}-\lambda_{1}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega)}\right)\right)\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{C_{2\beta}}{12}-\lambda_{1}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega)}\right)\right)\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\beta}\right)dx

(8.1)

for some
\lambda_{1}<\infty
, where we wrote thanks to inequality (
1.10
) of Theorem
1.3
, inequality (
1.8
) of Theorem
1.2
, and inequality (
1.12
) of Theorem
1.4
,

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}|\Delta v|^{2}dx=\frac{1}{6}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}|\Delta v|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}|\Delta v|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{12}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}|\Delta v|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|\nabla v|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx+\frac{2\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{4a}\right)}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx+\frac{C_{2\beta}}{12}\int_{\Omega_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\beta}\right)dx.

In particular, the following result holds true.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of extrinsic biharmonic maps
u_{k}:B(0,1)\rightarrow(M^{m},h)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Assume that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B(0,1))}+\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(B(0,1))}\right)<\infty.

Then, if
\rho_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}0
and
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B_{\alpha}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}}(0)
is a neck region of
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
such that for all
0<\alpha<\alpha_{0}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough, for all
v\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))

\displaystyle Q_{u_{k}}(v)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|\nabla v|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\rho_{k}}\right)}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx+\frac{C_{2\beta}}{12}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|v|^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\beta}\right)dx.

(8.2)

Proof.

Indeed, thanks to the strong energy quantization of Theorem
5.1
, we need only impose

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{k}(2\alpha_{0}))}+\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\Omega_{k}(2\alpha_{0}))}\leq\frac{1}{4\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(2\alpha_{0}))}+\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega_{k}(2\alpha_{0}))}\leq\min\left\{\pi^{2},\frac{C_{2\beta}}{24}\right\}

and the inequality follows from (
8.1
).
∎

In particular, if

\displaystyle\omega_{a,b}(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,x\in B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\\
&\frac{1}{b^{4}}\left(1+\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,x\in B(0,1)\setminus\overline{B}_{b}(0)\\
&\frac{1}{a^{4}}\left(1+\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,x\in B(0,a)\\
\end{aligned}\right.,

then for all
v\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{u_{k}}(v)\geq\lambda_{0}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\left(|\nabla v|^{2}+|v|^{2}\right)\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}dx,

where

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}=\min\left\{\frac{1}{12},\pi^{2},\frac{C_{2\beta}}{12}\right\}.

8.2
Weighted Diagonalisation of the Second Derivative

Recall that by (
1.2
), we have

\displaystyle Q_{u}(w)=\int_{B(0,1)}\bigg\{|\Delta w|^{2}+\langle A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u),(\Delta A_{u})(w,w)+2(\nabla A_{u})(\nabla w,w)+2\,A_{u}(\nabla w,\nabla w)+2\,A_{u}(w,\Delta w)\rangle

\displaystyle-2\langle\langle\Delta u,\nabla P_{u}\rangle,(\nabla A_{u})(w,w)+2\,A_{u}(\nabla w,w)\rangle+\langle\langle\Delta(P_{u}),\Delta u\rangle,A_{u}(w,w)\rangle\bigg\}dx.

(8.3)

Let
S:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be the shape operator such that for all
x\in\Sigma
and
X,Y,Z\in T_{x}\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\langle A_{x}(X,Y),Z\rangle=\langle S_{x}(Z)X,Y\rangle.

Likewise, for all
1\leq i,j\leq 2
there exists shape operators
S^{i}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
S^{i,j}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that for all
x\in\Sigma
and
X,Y,Z\in T_{x}\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\langle(\partial_{x_{i}}A_{x})(X,Y),Z\rangle=\langle S_{x}^{i}(Z)X,Y\rangle\qquad\text{and}\qquad\langle(\partial_{x_{i},x_{j}}^{2}A_{x})(X,Y),Z\rangle=\langle S_{x}^{i,j}(Z)X,Y\rangle.

Therefore, we can rewrite the second derivative as

\displaystyle Q_{u}(w)=\int_{B(0,1)}\langle\mathscr{L}_{u}(w),w\rangle\,dx,

where
\mathscr{L}_{u}
is the elliptic, fourth-order,
self-adjoint operator

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{u}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\,S_{u}^{i,i}(A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u))+2\sum_{i=1}^{2}S_{u}^{i}(A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u))\partial_{x_{i}}(\,\cdot\,)-2\dive\left(S_{u}(A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u))\nabla(\,\cdot\,)\right)

\displaystyle+S_{u}(A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u))\Delta(\,\cdot\,)+\dive\left(S_{u}(A_{u}(\nabla u,\nabla u)\nabla(\,\cdot\,)\right)

\displaystyle-2\sum_{i=1}^{2}S_{u}^{i}(\langle\Delta u,\partial_{x_{i}}P_{u}\rangle-4\sum_{i=1}^{2}S_{u}\left(\langle\langle\Delta u,\partial_{x_{i}}P_{u}\rangle,\right\rangle)\partial_{x_{i}}(\,\cdot\,)+S_{u}(\langle\Delta u,\Delta(P_{u})\rangle.

In particular, if

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\alpha,k}=\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{u_{k}},

we have

\displaystyle Q_{u_{k}}(w)=\int_{B(0,1)}\langle w,\mathscr{L}_{\alpha,k}w\rangle\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}\,dx.

We can therefore define for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
the weighted associated eigenspace as

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\alpha,k}(\lambda)=W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1))\cap\left\{u:\mathscr{L}_{\alpha,k}u=\lambda\,u\right\}.

We take function that vanish on the boundary since in applications, one would consider closed (compact with boundary) manifolds, on which one can integrate by parts without creating boundary components. Alternatively, one could directly work on a closed manifold, but that would not bring us anything new from the analysis viewpoint for we would consider a sequence from a
fixed

4
-dimensional manifold (there is no equivalent to Deligne-Mumford compactification in dimension
4
).
Then, following the proof of
[
25
, Lemma IV.3]
, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 8.2
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\alpha<1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(u_{k})=\dim\bigoplus_{\lambda<0}\mathscr{E}_{\alpha,k}(\lambda).

(8.4)

The fundamental lemma that allows us to take weak limits of an appropriately normalised sequence of eigenfunctions is the following (see
[
25
, Lemma IV.4]
).

Lemma 8.3
.

There exists
\alpha_{0}>0
and
0<\mu_{0}<\infty
, and for all
0<\alpha<\alpha_{0}
, there exists a sequence
\left\{\mu_{\alpha,k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\mu_{0})
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mu_{\alpha,k}=0.

Furthermore, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}\mathscr{E}_{\alpha,k}(\lambda)>0\implies\lambda\geq-\mu_{\alpha,k}\geq-\mu_{0}.

Proof.

Thanks to the pointwise bound of Theorem
7.5
, we have for all
0<\alpha<\alpha_{0}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}u_{k}(x)|^{2}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(2\alpha))}+\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\right)\,\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}\qquad\text{for all}\;\,x\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha)

\displaystyle|\nabla u_{k}(x)|^{4}\leq C\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(2\alpha))}+\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\right)\,\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}\qquad\text{for all}\;\,x\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha)

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left\|\frac{|\nabla^{2}u_{k}|^{2}}{\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}+\left\|\frac{|\nabla u_{k}|^{4}}{\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\right)=0.

Likewise, using the strong convergence of
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
towards
u_{\infty}
in
B_{1}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha}(0)
and the strong convergence in bubble domains, we show as in
[
25
]
that

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left\|\frac{|\nabla^{2}u_{k}|^{2}}{\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B(0,1))}+\left\|\frac{|\nabla u_{k}|^{4}}{\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B(0,1))}\right)=0.

Therefore, letting

\displaystyle\mu_{\alpha,k}=\left\|\frac{|\nabla^{2}u_{k}|^{2}}{\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B(0,1))}+\left\|\frac{|\nabla u_{k}|^{4}}{\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B(0,1))},

we have in particular

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}u_{k}|^{2}+|\nabla u_{k}|^{4}\leq\mu_{\alpha,k}\,\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}

Therefore, if
w\in\mathscr{E}_{\alpha,k}(\lambda)
and
\lambda<0
, we get

\displaystyle Q_{u_{k}}(w)=\int_{B(0,1)}\langle w,\mathscr{L}_{\alpha,k}w\rangle\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}=\lambda\int_{B(0,1)}|w|^{2}\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}dx,

while by (
1.3
) and (
8.1
) (applied to
B(0,1)
instead, using the previous upper bound)

\displaystyle Q_{u_{k}}(w)\geq\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{|\nabla w|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx-\mu_{\alpha,k}\int_{B(0,1)}|w|^{2}\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}\,dx\geq-\mu_{\alpha,k}\int_{B(0,1)}|w|^{2}\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}\,dx,

which shows that
\lambda\geq-\mu_{\alpha,k}
.
∎

We also need the following elementary generalisation of
[
29
, Lemma 1.4.5]
(see also
[
25
, Lemma B.1]
).

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
u:B(0,1)\rightarrow(M^{m},h)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a smooth extrinsic biharmonic map, and let
\omega\in C^{\infty}(B(0,1)\setminus\left\{0\right\})
such that for some
0<\alpha<1

\displaystyle 0<\omega_{0}\leq\omega(x)\leq\frac{C_{0}}{|x|^{4(1-\beta)}}\quad\text{and}\quad|\nabla^{l}\omega(x)|\leq\frac{C_{l}}{|x|^{4(1-\beta)}+l}\qquad\text{for all}\;\,l\in\mathbb{N},

where
\omega_{0},\left\{C_{l}\right\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
. Denote

\displaystyle L^{2}_{\omega}=L^{2}(B(0,1))\cap\left\{u:\int_{B(0,1)}u^{2}\omega\,dx<\infty\right\}.

Consider the operator
\mathscr{L}_{\omega}=\omega\mathscr{L}_{u}
, where
\mathscr{L}_{u}=\Delta^{2}+\cdots
is the fourth-order elliptic operator such that

\displaystyle Q_{u}(w)=\int_{B(0,1)}\langle w,\mathscr{L}_{u}w\rangle dx

for all
W^{2,2}
variation
w:B(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, there exists a Hilbertian base of
L^{2}_{\omega}(B(0,1))
made of eigenvectors of
\mathscr{L}_{\omega}
whose eigenvalues satisfy

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots\lambda_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\infty.

Finally, if

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\alpha,\infty}^{0}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\leq 0}\mathscr{E}_{\alpha,\infty}(\lambda),

where

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\alpha,\infty}(\lambda)=W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1))\cap\left\{u:\omega_{0,\alpha}^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{u_{\infty}}w=\lambda\,w\right\},

we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}\left(\mathscr{E}_{\alpha,\infty}\right)\leq\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(u_{\infty})+\mathrm{Null}_{E}(u_{\infty}).

We can finally move to the proof of the main theorem
1.1
. Let us recall it for the convenience of the reader.

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}:B(0,1)\rightarrow(M^{m},h)
be a sequence of biharmonic maps such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}E(u_{k})<\infty.

Then, if
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
bubble converges towards
(u_{\infty},v_{1},\cdots,v_{N})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(u_{\infty})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(v_{i})\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Ind}_{E}(u_{k})\leq\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Ind}_{E}^{0}(u_{k})\leq\mathrm{Ind}_{E}^{0}(u_{\infty})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{Ind}_{E}^{0}(v_{i}),

where
\mathrm{Ind}_{E}^{0}=\mathrm{Ind}+\mathrm{Null}
.

Proof.

We only treat the case
N=1
where there is a single bubble. Up to translation, we assume that
0\in B(0,1)
is the point of concentration. As in
[
25
, Lemma IV.6]
, introduce the following infinite-dimensional sphere:

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{\alpha,k}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\leq 0}\mathscr{E}_{\alpha,k}(\lambda)\cap\left\{w:\int_{B(0,1)}|w|^{2}\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}dx=1\right\}.

Let
w_{k}\in\mathscr{S}_{\alpha,k}
and
\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
be such that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\alpha,k}w_{k}=\lambda_{k}\,w_{k}.

For
k
large enough, we have

\displaystyle Q_{u_{k}}(w_{k})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{B(0,1)}\left(|\Delta w_{k}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla w_{k}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx-\mu_{\alpha,k}\int_{B(0,1)}|w_{k}|^{2}\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}\,dx

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{B(0,1)}\left(|\Delta w_{k}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla w_{k}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx-\mu_{\alpha,k}\int_{B(0,1)}|w_{k}|^{2}\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}\,dx.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle Q_{u_{k}}(w_{k})=\lambda_{k}\int_{B(0,1)}|w_{k}|^{2}\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k},\alpha}\,dx=\lambda_{k}\geq-\mu_{0},

which shows that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\left(|\Delta w_{k}|^{2}+\frac{|\nabla w_{k}|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)dx\leq\mu_{0}.

Therefore,
\left\{w_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1))
, which implies that up to a subsequence,

\displaystyle w_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}w_{\infty}\quad\text{in}\;\,W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1))\qquad\text{and}\qquad u_{k}(\delta_{k}x)\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}v_{\infty}\quad\text{in}\;\,W^{2,2}_{0}(B(0,1)).

Thanks to our stability inequality
8.2
, as in
[
29
]
, the same cutoff argument shows that we have either
u_{\infty}\neq 0
or
v_{\infty}\neq 0
. The rest of the proof is identical to the proofs of
[
25
, Lemma IV.6]
and
[
29
, p. 78]
and we omit it.
∎

9
Appendix

9.1
Basic Properties of Lorentz Spaces

Most of the presentation here overlaps with
[
35
]
. Another useful reference is
[
16
]
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a fixed measured space, and fix a measurable function
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Define

\displaystyle f_{\ast}(t)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\lambda>0:\mu(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>\lambda\right\}\leq t\right\}

\displaystyle f_{\ast\ast}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\ast}(s)ds

and for all
1<p<\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty
, we define

\displaystyle\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{q}{p}}f_{\ast}^{q}(t)\frac{dt}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{q}{p}}f_{\ast\ast}^{q}(t)\frac{dt}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

where for
q=\infty
, we define instead

\displaystyle\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,\infty}(X)}=\sup_{t>0}\left(t^{p}f_{\ast}(t)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,\infty}(X)}=\sup_{t>0}\left(t^{p}f_{\ast\ast}(t)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

The basic property in the theory of Lorentz spaces is that
\left\|\,\cdot\,\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}
is a norm for all
1<p<\infty
and for all
1\leq q\leq\infty
, while
\left|\,\cdot\,\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}
is only a semi-norm in general. However, it is usually easier to work with
\left|\,\cdot\,\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}
, and that is why this semi-norm will keep appearing in the proofs. Let us recall that if
q=1
, then
\left|\,\cdot\,\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,1}(X)}
is a norm and we have (see the appendix of
[
30
]
)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,1}(X)}=\frac{p}{p-1}|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,1}(X)}=\frac{p^{2}}{p-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>t\right\}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}dt.

(9.1)

Likewise, we have
L^{p,p}(X)=L^{p}(X)
, and
\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,p}(X)}=\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(X)}
.
Furthermore, using Fubini’s theorem, it is easy to show that for all
1<p<\infty
and for all
1\leq q<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}=p^{\frac{1}{q}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q}\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>t\right\}\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\frac{dt}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

Finally, the two quantities can be estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}.

(9.2)

Proposition 9.1
.

For all
1<p<\infty
and for all
1\leq q\leq r\leq\infty
, we have a continuous embedding
L^{p,r}(X)\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{p,q}(X)
, and for all
f\in L^{p,q}(X)
, the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,r}(X)}\leq\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{r}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}.

(9.3)

Proof.

We have by (
9.2
)

\displaystyle t^{\frac{1}{p}}f_{\ast}(t)=\left(\frac{q}{p}\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{q}{p}}f_{\ast}^{q}(t)\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{q}{p}}f_{\ast}^{q}(s)\frac{ds}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)},

which shows that

\displaystyle\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)},

and

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,\infty}(X)}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}.

Now, assume that
1\leq q<\infty
. Then, we have by an immediate interpolation

\displaystyle\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,r}(X)}\leq\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}^{\frac{q}{r}}\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,\infty}(X)}^{1-\frac{q}{r}}\leq\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{r}}\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{r}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)},

which concludes the proof of the Proposition by another application of (
9.2
).
∎

9.2
An Averaging Lemma for Lorentz Spaces

Let
d\geq 2
and
0<a<b<\infty
, and define
I=[a,b]
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For all
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
define
\overline{f}:[a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle f(r)=\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B(0,r))}=\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|f|^{2}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(9.4)

Thanks to the co-area formula, we have

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}|\overline{f}(r)|^{2}d\mathscr{L}^{1}(r)=\int_{\Omega}|f(x)|^{2}d\mathscr{L}^{d}(x).

We goal of this section is to generalise this estimate to the
L^{2,1}
norm.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
d\geq 2
and
0<a<b<\infty
, and define
I=[a,b]
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For all
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
define
\overline{f}:[a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for all
a\leq r\leq b
by

\displaystyle\overline{f}(r)=\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B(0,r))}=\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|f|^{2}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(9.5)

Then, provided that
f\in L^{2,1}(\Omega)
, we have
\overline{f}\in L^{2,1}([a,b])
and

\displaystyle\left\|\overline{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(I)}\leq 2^{\frac{d}{4}}\sqrt{\beta(d)}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)},

(9.6)

where
\beta(d)=\mathscr{H}^{d-1}(S^{d-1})=\dfrac{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)}
.

Remark 9.3
.

If
c_{d}
is the constant of the inequality (
9.6
), we deduce in particular that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} c_{2}&=2\sqrt{\pi}\\
c_{3}&=2^{\frac{7}{4}}\sqrt{\pi}\\
c_{4}&=2\pi\sqrt{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

Proof.

Assume that
f=c\,\mathbf{1}_{A}
for a measurable set
A
. Then, we trivially have

\displaystyle f_{\ast}(t)=c\,\mathbf{1}_{[0,\mathscr{L}^{n}(A)]}(t),

which yields

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{t}f_{\ast}(t)\frac{dt}{t}=2\,c\int_{0}^{\mathscr{L}^{n}(A)}\sqrt{t}\frac{dt}{t}=4\,c\sqrt{\mathscr{L}^{n}(A)}.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{n}\left([a,b]\cap\left\{r:\overline{f}(r)>\lambda\right\}\right)=\mathscr{L}^{n}\left([a,b]\cap\left\{r:c\sqrt{\mathscr{H}^{d-1}\left(A\cap\partial B(0,r)\right)}>\lambda\right\}\right).

Therefore,
\mathscr{L}^{n}\left([a,b]\cap\left\{r:\overline{f}(r)>\lambda\right\}\right)>t
implies by the co-area formula that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{n}(A)=\int_{a}^{b}\mathscr{H}^{d-1}\left(A\cap\partial B(0,r)\right)\,dr\geq\left(\frac{\lambda}{c}\right)^{2}\,t.

Therefore, we deduce the crude estimate

\displaystyle\overline{f}_{\ast}(t)\leq c\sqrt{\frac{\mathscr{L}^{n}(A)}{t}}\mathrm{1}_{[0,\mathscr{L}^{1}(I)]}(t).

(9.7)

In order to refine this inequality, we restict to a simpler class of sets: balls. If
A=B(p,R)\subset\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
, then

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{d-1}(\partial B(0,r)\cap A)\leq\beta(d)(2R)^{d-1}\mathbf{1}_{[|p|-R,|p|+R]}(r)

where
\beta(d)=\mathscr{H}^{d-1}(S^{d-1})
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{1}\left([a,b]\cap\left\{r:\overline{f}(r)>\lambda\right\}\right)\leq\left\{\begin{aligned} &2R\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,\lambda<c\sqrt{\beta(d)(2R)^{d-1}}\\
&0\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,\lambda\geq c\sqrt{\beta(d)(2R)^{d-1}}.\end{aligned}\right.

In other words, we have

\displaystyle\overline{f}_{\ast}(t)\leq c\sqrt{\beta(d)(2R)^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{[0,2R]}(t).

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\left\|\overline{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(I)}\leq 2\int_{0}^{2R}c\sqrt{\beta(d)(2R)^{d-1}}\sqrt{t}\frac{dt}{t}=4\,c\sqrt{\beta(d)(2R)^{d-1}}\sqrt{2R}=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\,4\,c\sqrt{\mathscr{L}^{n}(A)}=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

Using both inequalities, we deduce that

\displaystyle\overline{f}_{\ast}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\min\left\{c\sqrt{\beta(d)(2R)^{d-1}},c\sqrt{\frac{\beta(d)R^{d}}{t}}\right\}\mathrm{1}_{[0,2R]}(t)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &c\sqrt{\beta(d)(2R)^{d-1}}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,0\leq t\leq\sqrt{\frac{R}{2^{d-1}}}\\
&c\sqrt{\frac{\beta(d)R^{d}}{t}}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,\sqrt{\frac{R}{2^{d-1}}}<t<2R\\
&0\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,t\geq 2R\end{aligned}\right.

In general, write

\displaystyle f=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\,\mathbf{1}_{A_{i}},

where without loss of generality, we assume that
0<c_{1}<c_{2}<\cdots<c_{n}<\infty
and
A_{1},\cdots,A_{n}
are pairwise disjoint sets. If
c_{0}=0
, then for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{f}(t)=\mathscr{L}^{n}(\Omega\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>t\right\})=\sum_{j=i}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})\qquad\text{for all}\;\,c_{i-1}\leq t<c_{i}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}=4\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\lambda_{f}(t)}\,dt=4\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{\sum_{j=i}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})}.

(9.8)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle f_{\ast}(t)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &c_{n}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,0<t<\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{n})\\
&c_{n-1}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{n})\leq t<\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{n})+\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{n-1})\\
&\vdots\\
&c_{1}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,\sum_{i=2}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{i})\leq t<\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{i})\\
&0\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,t\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{i}).\end{aligned}\right.

(9.9)

Therefore, we recover

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{t}\,f_{\ast}(t)\frac{dt}{t}=4c_{n}\sqrt{\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{n})}+4c_{n-1}\left(\sqrt{\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{n})+\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{n-1})}-\sqrt{\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{n})}\right)

\displaystyle+\cdots+4c_{1}\left(\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{i})}-\sqrt{\sum_{i=2}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{i})}\right)

\displaystyle=4\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\left(\sqrt{\sum_{j=i}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})}-\sqrt{\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})}\right)=4\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{\sum_{j=i}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})}.

(9.10)

On the other hand, the previous argument with the co-area formula shows that

\displaystyle\overline{f}_{\ast}(t)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{i})}\,\mathbf{1}_{[0,\mathscr{L}^{1}(I)]}(t).

Now, let us show by induction that for all
n\geq 1

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}|A_{j}|}\geq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}|A_{i}|},

(9.11)

where we wrote for simplicity of notation
|A_{i}|=\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{i})
. Notice that it is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\left(\sqrt{\sum_{j=i}^{n}|A_{i}|}-\sqrt{\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}|A_{j}|}\right)\geq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}|A_{i}|}.

(9.12)

First, notice that the elementary inequality

\displaystyle c_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{j}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\leq\sqrt{j}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{j}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

shows that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|A_{j}|=\sum_{j=1}^{n}|A_{j}|\sum_{i=1}^{j}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})^{2}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{c_{j}^{2}}{j}|A_{j}|

which does not suffice for our purpose. The case
n=1
is trivial, so let us treat the cases
n=2
and
n=3
. Let us simplify the notations further by writing
D_{i}=|A_{i}|

For
n=2
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{\sum_{j=i}^{2}D_{j}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(c_{1}\sqrt{D_{1}+D_{2}}+(c_{2}-c_{1})\sqrt{D_{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})+(c_{1}^{2}+c_{2}^{2}-2c_{2}c_{2})D_{2}+2c_{1}(c_{2}-c_{1})\sqrt{D_{2}}\sqrt{D_{1}+D_{2}}

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{2}(D_{1}+2D_{2})+c_{2}^{2}D_{2}-2c_{1}c_{2}D_{2}+2c_{1}(c_{2}-c_{1})\sqrt{D_{2}}\sqrt{D_{1}+D_{2}}

\displaystyle\geq c_{1}^{2}(D_{1}+\cancel{2D_{2}})+c_{2}^{2}D_{2}-\cancel{2c_{1}c_{2}D_{2}}+2c_{1}(\cancel{c_{2}}-\cancel{c_{1}})D_{2}

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{2}D_{1}+c_{2}^{2}D_{2}.

For
n=3
, we get

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{\sum_{j=i}^{3}D_{j}}\right)^{2}=\left(c_{1}\sqrt{D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}}+(c_{2}-c_{1})\sqrt{D_{2}+D_{3}}+(c_{3}-c_{2})\sqrt{D_{3}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{2}(D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3})+(c_{1}^{2}+c_{2}^{2}-2c_{2}c_{2})(D_{2}+D_{3})+(c_{2}^{2}+c_{3}^{2}-2c_{2}c_{3})D_{3}

\displaystyle+2c_{1}(c_{2}-c_{1})\sqrt{D_{2}+D_{3}}\sqrt{D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}}+2c_{1}(c_{3}-c_{2})\sqrt{D_{3}}\sqrt{D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}}

\displaystyle+2(c_{2}-c_{1})(c_{3}-c_{2})\sqrt{D_{3}}\sqrt{D_{2}+D_{3}}

\displaystyle=c_{1}^{2}(D_{1}+2D_{2}+2D_{3})+c_{2}^{2}(D_{2}+2D_{3})+c_{3}^{2}D_{3}-2c_{1}c_{2}(D_{2}+D_{3})-2c_{2}c_{3}D_{3}

\displaystyle+2c_{1}(c_{2}-c_{1})\sqrt{D_{2}+D_{3}}\sqrt{D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}}+2c_{1}(c_{3}-c_{2})\sqrt{D_{3}}\sqrt{D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}}

\displaystyle+2(c_{2}-c_{1})(c_{3}-c_{2})\sqrt{D_{3}}\sqrt{D_{2}+D_{3}}

\displaystyle\geq c_{1}^{2}(D_{1}+\cancel{2D_{2}+2D_{3}})+c_{2}^{2}(D_{2}+\cancel{2D_{3}})+c_{3}^{2}D_{3}-2c_{1}c_{2}(D_{2}+D_{3})-2c_{2}c_{3}D_{3}

\displaystyle+2c_{1}(c_{2}-\cancel{c_{1}})(D_{2}+D_{3})+\cancel{2c_{1}(c_{3}-c_{2})D_{3}}+2(c_{2}-\cancel{c_{1}})(c_{3}-\cancel{c_{2}})D_{3}=c_{1}^{2}D_{1}+c_{2}^{2}D_{2}+c_{3}^{2}D_{3}.

In general, we have

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{\sum_{j=i}^{n}D_{j}}\right)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i-1}^{2}+c_{i}^{2}-2c_{i-1}c_{i})^{2}\sum_{j=i}^{n}D_{j}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{\sum_{j=i}^{n}D_{j}}\sum_{k=i+1}^{n}(c_{k}-c_{k-1})\sqrt{\sum_{l=k+1}^{n}D_{l}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}\left(D_{i}+2\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}D_{j}\right)-2\sum_{i=2}^{n}c_{i-1}c_{i}\sum_{j=i}^{n}D_{j}+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{\sum_{j=i}^{n}D_{j}}\sum_{k=i+1}^{n}(c_{k}-c_{k-1})\sqrt{\sum_{l=k}^{n}D_{l}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}\left(D_{i}+2\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}D_{j}\right)-2\sum_{i=2}^{n}c_{i-1}c_{i}\sum_{j=i}^{n}D_{j}+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sum_{k=i+1}^{n}(c_{k}-c_{k-1})\sum_{l=k}^{n}D_{l}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}\left(D_{i}+2\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}D_{j}\right)-2\sum_{i=2}^{n}c_{i-1}c_{i}\sum_{j=i}^{n}D_{j}+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})(c_{i+1}-c_{i})\sum_{l=i+1}^{n}D_{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}D_{i},

which concludes the proof of the inequality (
9.11
). Notice that using that
\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\in L^{2,\infty}([a,b])
, this bound only furnishes up the trivial inequality

\displaystyle\left\|\overline{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(I)}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)},

and we will have to refine our bound.

To simplify calculations (the general case seems to be a combinatorial nightmare), we will make two further reductions reductions. Notice that for all measurable function
f\in L^{1}(\Omega)
, if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{N_{n}}
are the open, disjoint, dyadic cubes with sides parallel to the coordinate axes, congruent to
[-\frac{1}{2n},\frac{1}{2n}]^{d}
such that
Q_{i}\subset\Omega
chosen such that

\displaystyle\frac{n^{d}\mathscr{L}^{d}(\Omega)}{N_{n}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}1,

defining

\displaystyle f_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{n}}\left({\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int}_{\mkern-10.0muQ_{i}}f\,d\mathscr{L}^{d}\right)\mathbf{1}_{Q_{i}},

we have

\displaystyle f_{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}f\qquad\mathscr{L}^{d}\;\,\text{almost everywhere.}

In particular, if
f
is bounded (using that
\Omega
has finite measure), by the dominated convergence theorem of Lebesgue, we deduce that for all
1<p<\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty

\displaystyle\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(\Omega)}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(\Omega)}.

Furthermore, by the embedding
L^{p,q}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{p,\infty}(\Omega)
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{4}(\Omega\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>A\right\})\leq c_{p,q}\frac{\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(\Omega)}}{A^{p}}\underset{A\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}0,

which implies that for all
t>0

\displaystyle(f-f\mathbf{1}_{A})_{\ast}(t)\leq\mathscr{L}^{4}(\Omega\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>A\right\})\underset{A\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}0,

and since
(f-f\mathbf{1}_{A})_{\ast}(t)\leq f_{\ast}(t)
, another application of Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem shows that
L^{p,q}\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
is dense in
L^{p,q}(\Omega)
, and finally, that it suffices to show our inequality for
f_{n}
as above by Fatou’s lemma for Lorentz spaces. Notice that alternatively, since
f_{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}f
in all
L^{p}
space for
1\leq p<\infty
provided that
f_{n}\in L^{p}\cap L^{\infty}
, interpolation theorem (here, simply Hölder’s inequality for Lorentz spaces) shows that
f_{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}f
in
L^{p,q}
for all
1<p<\infty
and
1\leq q\leq\infty
, where
f\in L^{p,q}\cap L^{\infty}
.

Therefore, the situation is reduced to the case where
A_{1},\cdots,A_{n}
are dyadic cubes of equal length
R>0
. Notice that for all
a<r<b
, since the cubes are disjoint, we have

\displaystyle\overline{f}(r)=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}\mathscr{H}^{d-1}(\partial B(0,r)\cap A_{i})}

In particular, for all
t>0
, the function

\displaystyle\lambda_{\overline{f}}(t)=\mathscr{L}^{1}\left([a,b]\cap\left\{r:\overline{f}(r)>t\right\}\right)

is maximal if the supports of the functions
r\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathscr{H}^{d-1}(\partial B(0,r)\cap A_{i})
are disjoint. Therefore, without loss of generality, we can make this assumption. Furthermore, if for all
1\leq i\leq n
,
C_{i}
is the ball such that
\partial C_{i}
is the circumscribed sphere of
A_{i}
, then
C_{i}
is a ball of radius
\frac{R}{\sqrt{2}}
. In particular, we have by the previous estimate

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{1}\left([a,b]\cap\left\{r:\overline{f}(r)>\lambda\right\}\right)\leq\left\{\begin{aligned} &0\hskip 18.49988pt&&\text{for all}\;\,\lambda\geq c_{n}\sqrt{\beta(d)\left(R\sqrt{2}\right)^{d-1}}\\
&R\sqrt{2}\hskip 18.49988pt&&\text{for all}\;\,c_{n-1}\sqrt{\beta(d)\left(R\sqrt{2}\right)^{d-1}}\leq\lambda<c_{n}\sqrt{\beta(d)\left(R\sqrt{2}\right)^{d-1}}\\
&2R\sqrt{2}\hskip 18.49988pt&&\text{for all}\;\,c_{n-2}\sqrt{\beta(d)\left(R\sqrt{2}\right)^{d-1}}\leq\lambda<c_{n-1}\sqrt{\beta(d)\left(R\sqrt{2}\right)^{d-1}}\\
&\vdots\\
&(n-1)R\sqrt{2}\hskip 18.49988pt&&\text{for all}\;\,c_{1}\sqrt{\beta(d)\left(R\sqrt{2}\right)^{d-1}}\leq\lambda<c_{2}\sqrt{\beta(d)\left(R\sqrt{2}\right)^{d-1}}\\
&nR\sqrt{2}\hskip 18.49988pt&&\text{for all}\;\,0<\lambda<c_{1}\sqrt{\beta(d)\left(R\sqrt{2}\right)^{d-1}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(9.13)

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\overline{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(I)}

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\mathscr{L}^{1}\left([a,b]\cap\left\{r:\overline{f}(r)>\lambda\right\}\right)}\,d\lambda\leq 4\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{c_{i-1}\sqrt{\beta(d)\left(R\sqrt{2}\right)^{d-1}}}^{c_{i}\sqrt{\beta(d)\left(R\sqrt{2}\right)^{d-1}}}\sqrt{(n-i+1)\sqrt{2}R}\,d\lambda

\displaystyle=4\cdot 2^{\frac{d}{4}}\sqrt{\beta(d)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{(n-i-1)R^{d}},

(9.14)

while

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}=4\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{\sum_{j=i}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})}=4\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-c_{i-1})\sqrt{(n-i+1)R^{d}}

since
\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{i})=R^{d}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Therefore, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\overline{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(I)}\leq 2^{\frac{d}{4}}\sqrt{\beta(d)}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}.

(9.15)

Therefore, using the density of test functions in
L^{2,1}
(
[
11
, Theorem 1.4.13]
) and (
9.15
), the theorem is proven. Indeed, by what precedes, if
\left\{f_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is the dyadic approximation of a general
f\in L^{2,1}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)},

while

\overline{f_{n}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\overline{f}

\mathscr{L}^{4}
almost everywhere. Therefore, thanks to the Fatou lemma for Lorentz spaces (see
[
11
,
1
,
3
]
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\overline{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(I)}\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\overline{f_{n}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(I)}\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,2^{\frac{d}{4}}\sqrt{\beta(d)}\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)}=2^{\frac{d}{4}}\sqrt{\beta(d)}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega)},

which concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

We can extend this result to the case of
L^{2,q}
norms for all
1\leq q<\infty
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
1\leq q<\infty
, and
0<a<b<\infty
, and define
I=[a,b]
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For all
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
define
\overline{f}:[a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for all
a\leq r\leq b
by

\displaystyle\overline{f}(r)=\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B(0,r))}=\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|f|^{2}\,d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(9.16)

Then, provided that
f\in L^{2,q}(\Omega)
, we have
\overline{f}\in L^{2,q}(I)
and

\displaystyle|\overline{f}|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,q}(I)}\leq 2^{\frac{d}{4}}\sqrt{\beta(d)}\,|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,q}(\Omega)},

(9.17)

where
\beta(d)=\mathscr{H}^{d-1}(S^{d-1})=\dfrac{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)}
.

Proof.

If
f
is the previous indicator of Theorem
9.2
, we have for all
1<q<\infty

\displaystyle|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,q}}^{q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{q}{2}}f_{\ast}^{q}(t)\frac{dt}{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})}^{\sum_{j={i}}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})}t^{\frac{q}{2}}c_{i}^{q}\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{q}\left(\left(\sum_{j=i}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}-\left(\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(c_{i}^{q}-c_{i-1}^{q}\right)\left(\sum_{j=i}^{n}\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}.

In particular, if
\mathscr{L}^{n}(A_{j})=R^{d}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
, we get

\displaystyle\overline{f}_{\ast}(t)=\frac{2}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{q}\left(\left((n-i+1)R^{d}\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}-\left((n-i)R^{d}\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\right).

On the other hand, we have by the previous computation

\displaystyle\overline{f}_{\ast}(t)\leq\left\{\begin{aligned} &c_{n}\sqrt{\beta(d)(R\sqrt{2})^{d-1}}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,0<t<R\sqrt{2}\\
&c_{n-1}\sqrt{\beta(d)(R\sqrt{2})^{d-1}}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,R\sqrt{2}<t<2R\sqrt{2}\\
\vdots\\
&c_{1}\sqrt{\beta(d)(R\sqrt{2})^{d-1}}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,(n-1)R\sqrt{2}<t<nR\sqrt{2}\\
&0\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,t\geq nR\sqrt{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

(9.18)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle|\overline{f}|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,q}}^{q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{q}{2}}\overline{f}_{\ast}^{q}(t)\frac{dt}{t}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{(i-1)R\sqrt{2}}^{iR\sqrt{2}}t^{\frac{q}{2}}c_{n-i+1}^{q}\left(\beta(d)(R\sqrt{2})^{d-1}\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=2^{\frac{qd}{4}}\beta(d)^{\frac{q}{2}}\frac{2}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{n-i+1}^{q}\left((iR\sqrt{2})^{\frac{q}{2}}-((i-1)R\sqrt{2})^{\frac{q}{2}}\right)=2^{\frac{qd}{4}}\beta(d)^{\frac{q}{2}}|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(\Omega)}^{q}.

∎

Remark 9.5
.

1.

For all measured space
(X,\mu)
, for all
1<p<\infty
, for all
1\leq q\leq\infty
, and
\varphi\in L^{p,q}(X)
, we have (by
[
1
]

\displaystyle|\varphi|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}|\varphi|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)},

we get by (
9.17
)

\displaystyle\left\|\overline{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,q}(I)}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}2^{\frac{d}{4}}\sqrt{\beta(d)}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,q}(\Omega)}.

(9.19)

2.

For
q=\infty
, we trivially get by (
9.9
)

\displaystyle|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}=\sup_{t>0}\sqrt{t}f_{\ast}(t)=\max\left\{\sqrt{R^{d}}c_{n},\sqrt{2R^{d}}c_{n-1},\cdots,\sqrt{nR^{d}}c_{1}\right\},

while (
9.18
) shows that

\displaystyle|\overline{f}|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(I)}=\sup_{t>0}\sqrt{t}\,\overline{f}_{\ast}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\sqrt{R\sqrt{2}}\,c_{n}\sqrt{\beta(d)(R\sqrt{2})^{d-1}},\sqrt{2R\sqrt{2}}\,c_{n-1}\sqrt{\beta(d)(R\sqrt{2})^{d-1}},\cdots,\sqrt{nR\sqrt{2}}\,c_{1}\sqrt{\beta(d)(R\sqrt{2})^{d-1}}\right\}

\displaystyle=2^{\frac{d}{4}}\sqrt{\beta(d)}|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega)},

which should allow us to generalise the theorem for
q=\infty
. However, for
q=\infty
, simple functions are
not
dense, though
countable
linear combinations of simple functions
are
dense (
[
11
, Remark 1.4.14]
).

In fact, the result generalises to
L^{p,q}
for all
p>2
and
1\leq q\leq\infty
thanks to the Stein-Weiss interpolation theorem, as the map

\displaystyle T:f\mapstochar\rightarrow\overline{f}:r\mapstochar\rightarrow\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\partial B(0,r))}

is a sub-linear map that sends
L^{p}(\Omega)
into
L^{p}(I)
for all
2\leq p<\infty
, which implies that
T
is a bounded map from
L^{p,q}(\Omega)
into
L^{p,q}(I)
for all
2<p<\infty
and
1\leq q\leq\infty
. Indeed, we have by Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\overline{f}(r)^{p}dr

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|f|^{2}d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}dr\leq\int_{a}^{b}\left(\beta(d)r^{d-1}\right)^{\frac{\frac{p}{2}}{\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)^{\prime}}}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|f|^{p}d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}\right)dr

\displaystyle=\beta(d)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\int_{a}^{b}r^{\frac{(d-1)(p-2)}{2}}\left(\int_{\partial B(0,r)}|f|^{p}d\mathscr{H}^{d-1}\right)dr

\displaystyle\leq\beta(d)^{\frac{p-2}{2}}b^{\frac{(d-1)(p-2)}{2}}\int_{\Omega}|f|^{p}dx,

where we used that
\left(\dfrac{p}{2}\right)^{\prime}=\dfrac{p}{p-2}
.
Our main theorem allowed us to treat the limiting case
(p,q)=(2,1)
.

9.3
Stability of Lorentz Spaces Under Exponentiation

We will also need an elementary result on the stability of Lorentz under squaring.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a measured space and
1<p<\infty
and
1\leq q\leq\infty
. Then, for all
f\in L^{2p,2q}(X)
, we have
f^{2}\in L^{p,q}(X)
and

\displaystyle\left\||f|^{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2p,2q}(X)}^{2}.

Proof.

For all
0<t<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle(|f|^{2})_{\ast}(t)=\sup\left\{\lambda:\mu(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|^{2}>\lambda\right\}\leq t\right\}=\sup\left\{\lambda:\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|>\sqrt{\lambda}\right\}\right)\leq t\right\}=f_{\ast}^{2}(t)

by definition of
f_{\ast}(t)
. Therefore, we get for all
1\leq q<\infty

\displaystyle\left|\,|f|^{2}\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{q}{p}}(|f|^{2})^{q}_{\ast}(t)\frac{dt}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{2q}{2p}}f_{\ast}^{2q}(t)\frac{dt}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}=\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{2p,2q}(X)}^{2}

and

\displaystyle\left\||f|^{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}\left||f|^{2}\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}=\frac{p}{p-1}\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{2p,2q}(X)}^{2}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2p,2q}(X)}^{2},

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Likewise, one proves the following stability result.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
1<p<\infty
,
1\leq q\leq\infty
, and
\alpha>0
such that
\alpha p>1
and
\alpha q\geq 1
. For all measured space
(X,\mu)
and for all measurable function
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
f\in L^{\alpha p,\alpha q}(X)
. Then,
|f|^{\alpha}\in L^{p,q}(X)
and

\displaystyle\left\||f|^{\alpha}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\alpha p,\alpha q}(X)}^{\alpha}.

Proof.

As in previous lemma, we need only check the case
q<\infty
since one immediately gets
(|f|^{\alpha})_{\ast}(t)=f_{\ast}^{\alpha}(t)
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\left||f|^{\alpha}\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}=\left(\int_{0}^{t}t^{\frac{q}{p}}(|f|^{\alpha})_{\ast}^{q}(t)\frac{dt}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha q}{\alpha p}}f_{\ast}^{\alpha q}(t)\frac{dt}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}=\left|f\right|_{\mathrm{L}^{\alpha p,\alpha q}(X)}^{\alpha},

which concludes the proof of the lemma thanks to (
9.2
).
∎

9.4
An Improved Sobolev Embedding

For all
0<\alpha<d
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{|x|^{\alpha}}\right)_{\ast}(t)

\displaystyle=\inf\left((0,\infty)\cap\left\{\lambda:\mathscr{L}^{d}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap\left\{x:\frac{1}{|x|^{\alpha}}>\lambda\right\}\right)\leq t\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\inf\left((0,\infty)\cap\left\{\lambda:\frac{\beta(d)}{\lambda^{\frac{d}{\alpha}}}\leq t\right\}\right)=\left(\frac{\beta(d)}{t}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{d}}.

Therefore, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{|x|^{\alpha}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{d}{\alpha},\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\beta(d)^{\frac{\alpha}{d}}\sup_{t>0}\frac{1}{t^{1-\frac{\alpha}{d}}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{s^{\frac{\alpha}{d}}}=\frac{d}{d-\alpha}\beta(d)^{\frac{\alpha}{d}}.

(9.20)

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left\|\frac{1}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}=\frac{4}{3}\left(2\pi^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\\
&\left\|\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}=2\sqrt{2\pi^{2}}=2\pi\sqrt{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

(9.21)

Refer to
[
35
]
for the next construction and theorems. First, we introduce a function
\varphi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\varphi)\subset[\frac{1}{2},2]
and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle x=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{j}\varphi(2^{-j}x).

It suffices to take

\displaystyle\varphi(x)=x\left(\psi\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)-\psi(x)\right),

where
\psi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R})
is such that
\psi=1
on
\left[-\dfrac{1}{2},\dfrac{1}{2}\right]
and
\mathrm{supp}(\psi)\subset[-1,1]
.

Theorem 9.8
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a measured space. Let
1<p<\infty
and
1\leq q\leq p
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}\left(\frac{p\,2^{3q}}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left\|\left\{\left\|f_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(X)}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\right\|_{{l}^{q}(\mathbb{Z})},

where
f_{k}=2^{k}\varphi(2^{-k}|f|)
.

Proof.

We have thanks to a direct integration by parts

\displaystyle|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}^{q}=p\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{q}\left(\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|\geq\lambda\right\}\right)\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\leq p\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{2^{j}}^{2^{j+1}}\lambda^{q}\left(\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|\geq\lambda\right\}\right)\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\leq p\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|\geq 2^{j}\right\}\right)\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\int_{2^{j}}^{2^{j+1}}\lambda^{q-1}d\lambda=\frac{p(2^{q}-1)}{q}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{jq}\left(\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:|f(x)|\geq 2^{j}\right\}\right)\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(2^{q}-1)}{q}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{jq}\left(\sum_{k\geq j}\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:2^{k}\leq|f(x)|<2^{k+1}\right\}\right)\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{p(2^{q}-1)}{q}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{jq}\sum_{k\geq j}\left(\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:2^{k}\leq|f(x)|<2^{k+1}\right\}\right)\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(2^{q}-1)}{q}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:2^{k}\leq|f(x)|<2^{k+1}\right\}\right)\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{jq}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{j\leq k\right\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p\,2^{q}}{q}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{kq}\left(\mu\left(X\cap\left\{x:2^{k}\leq|f(x)|<2^{k+1}\right\}\right)\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}.

If
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
2^{k}\leq|f(x)|<2^{k+1}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle|f(x)|=\sum_{l=k-2}^{k+1}|f_{l}(x)|,

which shows by Markov inequality and Hölder’s inequality that

\displaystyle 2^{k}\left(\mu(X\cap\left\{x:2^{k}\leq|f(x)|<2^{k+1}\right\}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{X}\left(\sum_{l=k-2}^{k+1}|f_{l}|\right)^{p}d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\sum_{l=k-2}^{k+1}\left\|f_{l}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(X)}.

Finally, we deduce by by Hölder’s inequality that

\displaystyle|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}^{p}\leq\frac{p\,2^{q}}{q}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sum_{l=k-2}^{k+1}\left\|f_{l}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(X)}\right)^{q}\leq\frac{p\,2^{3q-2}}{q}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{l=k-2}^{k+1}\left\|f_{l}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(X)}^{q}\leq\frac{p\,2^{3q}}{q}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\|f_{l}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(X)}^{q}.

which implies that

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}|f|_{\mathrm{L}^{p,q}(X)}\leq\frac{p}{p-1}\left(\frac{p\,2^{3q}}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left\|\left\{\left\|f_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(X)}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\right\|_{{l}^{q}(\mathbb{Z})}.

∎

Theorem 9.9
.

Let
1\leq p<d
. There is a continuous embedding
W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{p^{\ast},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\ast},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\frac{p(d-1)}{d-p}\frac{p^{\ast}}{p^{\ast}-1}\left(\frac{p^{\ast}\,2^{3p}}{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\ast}}}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

(9.22)

where
p^{\ast}=\dfrac{dp}{d-p}
.

Proof.

The Sobolev inequality implies that for all
u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
1\leq p<d
(to get this specific constant, one follows the standard proof exposed in
[
2
, Chapitre
8
]
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\ast}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\frac{p(d-1)}{d-p}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Applying this inequality to
f_{k}
, we get

\displaystyle\left\|\left\{\left\|u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\ast}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\right\|_{{l}^{p}(\mathbb{Z})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{p(d-1)}{d-p}\left\|\left\{\left\|\nabla u_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\right\|_{{l}^{p}(\mathbb{Z})}=\frac{p(d-1)}{d-p}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|u_{k}|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(d-1)}{d-p}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

as
\displaystyle u=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{k}
. Therefore, We have

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\ast},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\frac{p(d-1)}{d-p}\frac{p^{\ast}}{p^{\ast}-1}\left(\frac{p^{\ast}\,2^{3p}}{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\ast}}}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

∎

Remark 9.10
.

Notice that the the inequality can also be explicitly rewritten in terms of
p
and
d
as

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\ast},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\frac{d(d-1)p^{2}}{(d-p)((d+1)p-d)}\left(\frac{d\,2^{3p}}{d-p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\ast}}}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

Corollary 9.11
.

There is a continuous embedding
W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\hookrightarrow L^{4,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, and for all
u\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\leq 14\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}.

(9.23)

Proof.

It follows immediately from Theorem
9.9
and the estimate

\displaystyle\frac{p(d-1)}{d-p}\frac{p^{\ast}}{p^{\ast}-1}\left(\frac{p^{\ast}\,2^{3p}}{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\ast}}}=\frac{2\cdot 3}{4-2}\frac{4}{3}\left(\frac{4\cdot 2^{6}}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}=8\cdot 2^{\frac{3}{4}}<14,

since
2^{11}=2048<2401=7^{4}
.
∎

9.5
Whitney Extension Lemmas

Notice that if
f\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the chain rule implies that

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}f(\varphi(x))

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\partial_{x_{k}}f(\varphi(x))\cdot\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi_{k}(x)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j},x_{i}}^{2}f(\varphi(x))

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{l},x_{k}}^{2}f(\varphi(x))\partial_{x_{j}}\varphi_{l}(x)\right)\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi_{k}(x)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{k}}f(\varphi(x))\partial_{x_{j},x_{i}}^{2}\varphi_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{k},x_{l}}^{2}f(\varphi(x))\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi_{k}(x)\partial_{x_{j}}\varphi_{l}(x)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{k}}f(\varphi(x))\partial_{x_{i},x_{j}}^{2}\varphi_{k}(x).

Therefore, we deduce that
